"I did," said the Director. "Yesterday. She said she'd come if you did." She put her spectacles on and looked at the top sheet, and wrote something short on it. "The one with the atlas said he'd write it all down whether I asked him to or not. I told him I'd rather he did it with my paper. He said he preferred his own."

---

When they went out the Director said, "Miss," and Callie stopped in the door.

The others went on down the stair. She could hear it ringing under their boots, Rook's slow and the others' slower to match him.

"The slab at marker six," said the Director. "How long?"

Callie thought about it. She thought about the bright dirt round the dropped corner, and the silt on the black water in the joints, and the crack that had not been there on the ninth day.

"I don't know," she said. "I read what's happened. Not what will."

The Director looked at her over her spectacles.

"That's more honest than my engineers," she said. "Thank you."

---

"Did you sign?" said her mother.

She was lying down now, on the bed by the stove, with the blanket over the window and the stick propped against the wall where she could reach it. She had slept for most of the afternoon. Katori had sat with her while Callie was on the second floor, and when Callie came in Katori had got up without a word and gone out to the step.

"No," said Callie.

"Tell me."

So Callie told her. She told the whole of it, once, in order, from the jar on the windowsill to the Director's spectacles, the way Pell told a road and the way her mother had told the gate nurse. She told the holders, and where the four had gone. She told *about four seasons*. She told Jab's terms and the woman with the good boots saying *I'll stop you myself*. She told Toren saying no. She told her own *I'll come to the yard. I won't sign.*

She did not tell about *the old plant*, because she did not know what it meant, and it was Toren's.

Her mother listened with her eyes shut.

"Is it spending yourself?" she said, when Callie had finished.

Callie thought about it. She made herself think about it properly, the way her mother would have wanted, without deciding the answer first.

"It's learning," she said. "I think. The woman with the boots won't let anybody spend anything till she's measured it."

"And after she's measured it?"

"I don't know."

Her mother opened her eyes.

"Then here's my rule," she said. "Since everybody in this place has rules. You go to your yard. You learn what they've got to teach you. And every day, you come home with something left." She turned her head on the pillow. "Not everything. I'm not a fool; I know what a yard's for. But something. Every day. And you show me."

"Show you how?"

Her mother held out her left hand, palm up, on the blanket.

Callie looked at it. Then she put her own hand in it.

She did not know what her mother could feel through it. She did not know if there was anything to feel. There were two fingers of cold clear water in the bottom of the well, and had been for days, and she had not spent a drop of them; she did not know if that was the kind of thing that came through a hand. But her mother's fingers closed on hers and held them, and she lay there with her eyes on Callie's face, and after a while she nodded.

"There," she said. "That's something left."

"Something left," said Callie.

---

Katori was on the step with her cap pulled down, watching the row.

Callie sat down beside her, a little apart. The lamp at the end of the row was lit, and it put its flat white circle down on the frozen concrete, and past it the dark went all the way to the berm.

"She said you'd come if I did," said Callie. "The Director."

"She asked me what it would take. I told her."

"Why?"

Katori was quiet for a while. Down the row, on the step of eleven, somebody was sitting in the dark: Rook, by the shape of the shoulders. He was not turning anything in his fingers tonight. His hands lay open on his knees and they were still.

"You told me on the step that you hadn't decided if you were most people," said Katori. "I'd like to be there when you do."

Callie looked at the lamp.

"What's the old plant?" she said.

Katori did not answer for a long time.

"I don't know," she said at last. "But the boy in eleven does. I watched his face." She got up. "It's his, Callie. He'll say it when he's ready. You of all people know how that goes."

She went in. Callie sat on the step until the lamp went out at the first hour, and then she sat in the dark a little longer, with her hands in her lap and two fingers of water in the well that she had not spent, and she did not count anything at all.

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-11.md

# Chapter 11 — Jab: The Floor

The review room was on the first floor, at the top of fourteen stairs, and Jab went up them alone.

Otto had offered to come. He had stood on the step of twelve at the eighth hour with his hands in his pockets, going red, and said he could sit on the stair outside and not say anything, if that was a help. Jab had said it was not. Then he had seen Otto's face and said it was, a bit, and Otto had sat on the bottom stair with his big hands between his knees, not saying anything, and Jab had gone up past him.

It was the same room Callie had told him about on the step last night: a long table, three chairs behind it, two in front. The man in the long coat on the left. A woman with a red face and a ledger on the right. In the middle, an old man with a white moustache and pale eyes, looking at the window.

Jab sat in one of the two chairs. The other stayed empty. He looked at it once, and then did not look at it again.

"Household review," said the woman with the ledger. "Yard C, second row, hut twelve. Conditional admittance, fourteen days, reviewable." She read down the page with her finger. "Household, two. Head of household, the brother, seventeen. The other member a clinical admission, aged four." She turned the page. "On the sheet, a Flask of hound ash, lifted the night of admission, less station. A red thread. A declaration of capability, referred." She stopped, and frowned, and read the next line to herself before she read it out. "A request from the Director's office for a medical assessment, on terms, the terms attached. And a four-season contract, offered on the third day, for guard work on the Sag line." She looked up. "Withdrawn."

"I'd like it written that I said no," said Jab.

The woman looked at him.

"It's withdrawn, sir. You don't need to—"

"I know it's withdrawn. Somebody withdrew it yesterday, in a room upstairs, without me." He kept his hands flat on his knees. "A man told me on a step that I could say no. He said nobody here says it out loud, because everybody's busy being reasonable. I'd like it on the sheet that I said it. Not that it went away."

The woman looked at the man in the long coat.

"Write it," said the man in the long coat.

She wrote it. It took her a moment, because she had to find a place on the page where it would go, and there did not seem to be one. In the end she wrote it in the margin, small.

---

The old man with the moustache turned from the window.

"How old's the boy?" he said. His voice was thin and dry, like paper being folded.

"Four."

"And you carried him how far?"

"Four hundred miles," said Jab. "About. I counted it in rests, not in miles. It was eighteen days."

The old man looked at him for a long time with his pale eyes.

"Hm," he said.

It went into Jab like a hook. It was the sound his father had made. It was exactly the sound, a short low note in the nose, the one his father made at a rapid he had not decided about yet, or a knot somebody else had tied, or a boy standing in front of him with a question. It meant *I've heard you and I'm not finished*. Nobody had made it at Jab in two months. He had not known how much he had been waiting for somebody to.

He found he had to look at the table for a while.

"Twenty-eight days," said the woman with the ledger, writing. "Conditional residence extended, reviewable. The board notes the Flask. The board notes the Director's request and the terms attached." She paused. "And the no."

"Thank you," said Jab.

On the bottom stair, Otto stood up so fast he nearly fell over.

"Twenty-eight," said Jab.

Otto thought about it. His lips moved.

"That's the forty-second day," he said.

"It is," said Jab, and found, for the first time in several days, that something in him was nearly laughing. "You're getting as bad as the rest of us."

---

The east shed leaked at the north end.

There was a bucket under the drip. Jab saw it as soon as he came in out of the afternoon, standing by itself on the concrete in the half-dark at the far end, with a small grey circle of water in the bottom of it and a drop coming down every few seconds from a seam in the tin roof. *Tick*, it went, into the water. He watched it for a moment longer than he meant to.

The rest of the shed was long and low and cold, with a stove at the near end that somebody had lit an hour ago and that had not yet decided to be warm. There was a cot against one wall with a grey blanket folded on it. There was a table with a hand scale on it, brass, with little pans, and a row of stoppered jars, and a small brass-framed glass with sand in it, and a book open at a clean page.

The woman with the good boots was sitting on the edge of the table. She had taken her oilskin off. Under it she wore the grey of the long building, with the sleeves rolled to the elbow, and on the outside of her left wrist there was the lump where a bone had knit wrong.

Rook was sitting on the step outside the door.

"You can hear from there," the woman had said to him, when they came across the yard together. "You can't come in."

"It's my—"

"It's not yours. It's his." She had not raised her voice. "Sit on the step, Elias. If I do anything you don't like, you can say so through the door. You've got a loud enough voice for it. I've heard it."

Rook had sat on the step.

Inside there were three other people. Otto, standing by the stove, looking at everything with great care, as if he might be asked about it afterward. Dessa, sitting on the cot with her bad leg out straight. And Wyck, leaning against the wall by the door with his strapped arm across his chest and his bar in his left hand, looking at nobody.

"I asked for three people who wouldn't lie to me," said the woman. "Elias sent these. I've no idea yet whether he was right." She looked at Jab. "Sit down. Tell me what it is. Not what you think it is. What it does."

---

He told her.

He had told it once on a card and once to a man on a step. He had never told it out loud to somebody who wrote it down while he talked, and he found it came out differently. It came out in order.

The lean. How it lived under his breastbone, a finger's width behind it, and leaned, neither up nor down, like a floor in an old house. How it leaned toward his brother when his brother was worse. How he had first felt it on the road, three nights running, with his hand on Tonk. How it was right four times in five, and when it was wrong it was wrong in the direction he wanted.

"So you don't ask it," she said, writing, "when you want an answer."

"No."

"Who told you that?"

"Nobody. I made it a rule." He looked at his hands. "It was wrong the second time I asked it. I'd wanted it to say he was better."

She wrote.

The warmth. He told the snow shelter: four stones in the fire, and the four stones packed round his brother's back and feet, and pushing at the thing, pushing like a man leaning on a door, and nothing; and then stopping, and it coming. He told the culvert on the north road. He told the tenth day in the ward, sitting with his hand on his brother's chest while the pin went red, and nothing coming at all.

"It doesn't come when I call it," he said. "It never has."

"And the bucket."

He looked up.

"You said it went out of you *like a bucket going dry*," she said. "On your card. The back of it. I've read it four times." She turned the pages of her book back to one that was already written on, and he saw his own words copied there in her small square hand. "Tell me about the bucket."

So he told her that too. The bottom he went down to at night to look at, the way you go back to a snare you know is empty. Dry at the gate. A little in it by the tenth day. Something in it now.

"How much?"

"Some."

"How much is some?"

He thought about it. "A hand," he said. "About a hand deep. If the bucket were a bucket."

"Good," said the woman, and wrote *a hand*, and underlined it. "That's the first number anybody's given me about Sustain in a year. Keep giving me numbers. They don't have to be right. They have to be yours."

"Sustain," said Jab.

She put the pencil down.

"That's what it's called on the register," she said. "The line with very little written on it. Sensing and support. It's the other side of the same door Ward's on, the magic door. Most people who've got it have got a little of it, a feeling in the hands, a knack with a fever. A few people have got more." She looked at him. "I don't know yet what you've got. I know you've got something that goes out of you into another body and keeps it going. I read Tonk's chart for the night of the north road. I've never seen one like it."

"You know his name."

"I read his chart. I read yours." She picked the pencil up. "I read everything, Jab. It's the only thing I'm better at than him."

On the step outside the door, Rook said nothing at all.

---

"Now I want to see it," she said. "The lean. I'm going to set it up. I don't want you to want it. I'd like you to be bored, if you can manage it."

"I don't know if I can."

"Try." She stood up. "Shut your eyes. I'm going to put your hand on three people, one after another. I want you to tell me which of them is worse. That's all. Not what's wrong. Just worse."

"It only does my brother."

"Has it ever been asked about anybody else?"

Jab opened his mouth and shut it.

"No," he said.

"Then you don't know that it only does your brother," said the woman. "You know you've only ever asked it about him." She took his wrist, lightly, the way the nurse at the north gate had taken the boy's. "Shut your eyes."

He shut them.

She put his hand flat on somebody's chest, over the breastbone, through a shirt. The chest was very big and very warm and went up and down slowly, like a horse asleep. There was a heart under it going steady and heavy. He waited. He did not want anything; it was easy not to want anything about a chest this size.

"Nothing," he said. "He's all right. Something's itching him. On the arm."

Otto's voice, surprised, a long way up: "It *is*."

"Don't talk," said the woman. She moved his hand.

The next chest was narrow, and still, and sat very straight. The heart in it was quick and even. He felt, in the floor under his breastbone, something like a wall in an old house that has always leaned a little and always will. Not moving. Just there.

"There's something old," he said. "Low down. The leg, I think. It's not getting worse. It's not getting anywhere. It's just there."

On the cot, Dessa said nothing. But he heard her let her breath out through her nose.

The woman moved his hand again.

This chest was broad and hard and held itself very still against the wall, the way you hold something still that doesn't want to be. The heart under it was going harder than it should for a boy leaning against a wall doing nothing. And the floor under Jab's breastbone tipped.

It tipped the way it tipped at the second hour when Tonk was burning. Not as far. Not nearly as far. But it went, all at once, and it went one way, toward the right side of the chest under his hand, up under the collarbone where the strapping crossed.

*Worse*, it said.

He opened his eyes.

"This one," he said. "Here." He did not move his hand. "It's worse. Something's moved in there that was meant to be still."

---

"I'm fine," said Wyck.

"Take your coat off," said the woman.

"I'm fine."

"Then it won't take long." She had not moved. "Coat off, Wyck, or I'll send for Keel, and she'll take it off you, and she won't ask."

Wyck looked at her. Then he looked at Jab. It was a look Jab had seen on boys at home when somebody's little sister had told their mother where they'd been. He put the bar down against the wall and got his coat off his good shoulder, one-handed, and let it drop.

The strapping had slipped. Even Jab could see it: the bands that should have crossed at the back were riding high, and one of them had worked loose, and under the shirt the line of the collarbone had a bump in it that it had not had on the road.

The woman looked at it for about four seconds without touching it.

"Otto," she said, "go to the long building and ask Mrs Lask for Doctor Keel. Tell her it's a collarbone that's moved under strapping. Tell her it's Wyck. She'll know."

Otto went. He went fast, for Otto.

"How long?" said the woman to Wyck.

Wyck said nothing.

"How long have you been leaning on it?"

"Three nights," said Wyck at last. "Against the wall. To see if it'd hold." He looked at the floor. "It doesn't move me. Nothing moves me. I wanted to know if that was still true."

"It's still true," said the woman. "Nothing moves you. Your collarbone moved you. They're different things." She turned her head. "You knew?"

Jab realised she was asking him.

"No," he said. "I didn't know anything. It told me."

---

Doctor Keel came across the yard with her sleeves already rolled and her hair flat on one side. She looked at the collarbone for eleven seconds, and at Wyck's face for three, and then she took the strapping off and put it back on again in about the time it took Jab to understand what she was doing, and Wyck made one sound in the middle of it and no others.

"Six weeks," she said. "From today. Not from the road. From today." She stood back. "Pain tolerance isn't healing. I'd have thought a boy who'd been told what he's got by a man who knows would know the difference. Nothing moves you? Fine. That bone doesn't care. Leave it alone."

"Yes."

"Say it like you mean it."

"Yes, Doctor Keel."

"Good." She looked at the woman with the good boots. "You found it?"

"He did."

Keel turned and looked at Jab properly, the way she had looked at him in the corridor on the first day, when she told him about the nine.

"Did he," she said. She did not say anything else. She went back out across the yard with her sleeves still rolled, and Jab watched her go, and did not know what her face had done.

Wyck put his coat back on. It took him a long time with the new strapping, and nobody helped him, because nobody in hut eleven helped Wyck with anything he could do himself. When it was on he picked up his bar and stood holding it.

"You could've said nothing," he said to Jab.

"No," said Jab. "I couldn't."

"Why not?"

"I don't lie to people about their bodies," said Jab. "I've got a rule about it."

Wyck looked at him for a long moment. Jab waited for whatever was coming.

"Fine," said Wyck, and went out, and sat down on the step beside Rook, and the two of them sat there side by side and did not say anything to each other at all.

---

"Warm my hand," said the woman.

She held it out. She had been outside, crossing the yard, and it was cold; he could see that from here, the fingers white at the ends. He took it in both of his.

He thought: *come on.*

He thought it before he could stop himself. He wanted it to come. He wanted it very badly, because a man in a long coat and a woman in spectacles and a doctor with her sleeves rolled had all looked at him in the last three days as if he were a thing they might want, and he wanted, for once, to be the thing. He wanted her to feel it and write it down.

Nothing came.

He stopped wanting it. He tried to. He sat with her cold hand in his and tried to let go of the reaching part, the leaning-on-a-door part, the way he had at the culvert. But the culvert had had his brother going grey under his hand, and this had a stranger's cold fingers and a book open on the table, and he could feel himself wanting it the whole time, underneath, like a stone in a boot.

Nothing came. Her hand stayed cold.

After a while he let go of it.

"I'm sorry," he said.

"Don't be." She was writing. "Good."

"Good?"

"It doesn't come on demand." She did not look up. "Every Sustain I've ever met that came on demand was lying, or was about to kill somebody, usually themselves. That's the first honest thing I've learned about you." She put the pencil down. "You felt Wyck because you didn't care about the answer. You didn't feel my hand because you did. You told me that yourself. *Ask it when you don't care.* That's not a superstition. It's a description."

"Of what?"

"Of the door," said the woman. "I'll know more when I've seen more. I don't know yet whether the lean and the warmth are one thing or two. I think they might be. I'm not going to say it till I've seen both of them do something I didn't set up."

---

"One more thing," she said, "and then you can go, because it's nearly visiting."

"You know about visiting."

"It's on your terms. The man in the coat wrote them down. I've got them here." She tapped the book. "Show me the bottom of your bucket."

He looked at her.

"Don't tip it," she said. "Don't spend anything. Just go down and look, the way you do at night. And tell me how deep."

So he went down.

He shut his eyes, because it was easier, and went down under his breastbone to the place it lived, and found the bucket. It was the same as it had been last night: a hand of something at the bottom, dark and still, not moving, not wanting anything.

"A hand," he said. "Same as I said."

"Now find the floor."

"The bottom?"

"No. Above the bottom." She said it slowly, the way you say a thing to somebody walking a plank. "The last place you could stop. The last place you could take your hand off whoever's under it, and still walk home, and still light that knife of yours in the morning if something came up out of a ditch. It's not empty. Empty's the bottom. It's above that. It's where you'd have to stop to be any use tomorrow. Find it."

He looked.

At first there was nothing there to find. There was a hand of dark in the bottom of a bucket and the sides of the bucket going up. Then, looking, he found that there was a place a little way up the inside, not far above the bottom, where he knew, without knowing how he knew, that if the dark went below it he would not be able to get up off the floor afterward. He had been below it. He had been below it at the culvert, sitting down in the ash with people moving around him like rain. He had been below it in the snow, with a hand that would not close.

It was like a line scratched on the inside of the bucket, low down, by somebody who had been there before him and known.

"There," he said. "About a finger from the bottom."

"That's your floor," said the woman. "You never go below it. Not for anybody."

He opened his eyes.

"Not for anybody?" he said.

"Especially not for anybody."

He did not say anything.

She waited. She waited quite a long time, with the pencil in her hand, looking at him. Then she wrote something in the book, and he saw what it was, because she turned the book so that he could.

*Floor: a finger. Did not say yes.*

"I'll write that down too," she said. "Go on. It's visiting."

---

Rook walked back across the yard with him as far as the long building. It took a while, at Rook's pace.

"She's good," said Rook.

"Are you?"

Rook thought about it seriously, which Jab had come to understand was the only way he thought about anything.

"At some of it," he said. "Not at that. At the other end of it."

"What's the other end?"

"The end where there's no shed," said Rook. He stopped at the corner of the long building and got his breath, with one hand on the wall. "She'll teach you where your floor is. I've never known where mine was. I've only ever found out afterward, lying down."

"You said to ask you again in a while," said Jab. "About what it is."

"I did."

"Is it a while?"

Rook looked at him. The sun was going down behind the long building and the light came round it flat and orange and lit one side of Rook's face.

"No," he said. "Not yet. She's right about one thing, and I'll say it to you because I won't say it to her. You've got to see both halves do something nobody set up. Then ask me."

---

The north yard had lines on it in the morning.

Jab came through at first light on his way to the ward, because it was the quickest way from twelve, and found Rook in the corner by the east shed with a bucket of whitewash and a long brush, painting. He was doing it very slowly, bent over, with one hand on his knee. He had painted three lines across the cinders so far, long and straight, from the shed wall out toward the berm, and was starting a fourth that went round the end of the sand heap.

"What are they?" said Jab.

"Lanes," said Rook, without straightening up. "One's for doing things. One's for falling over. One's the shed." He finished the fourth line and stood the brush in the bucket. "I don't know yet if that's the right way round. We'll find out."

By the time the sun was up there were people in the lanes.

Wyck came first, in his new strapping, with his face like a shut door, and sat down on the sand heap at the end of the second lane with his bar across his knees. He did not say anything. After a while Otto came and stood near him, and Wyck looked at Otto's feet and said, "You stand like a man waiting for a cart that isn't coming. Put your weight in your heels," and Otto did, and Wyck said, "Better," and that was the second lane started.

Dessa came with a book under her arm. It was a big one, thick, with a cracked leather spine and ruled pages, and Pell came behind her looking pleased with himself in a way that told Jab exactly whose spare book it had been. Dessa sat down at a table somebody had carried out of the shed and set against its wall, and opened the book to the first page, and sat looking at it with a pencil in her hand.

Katori came with Callie, and stood where she could see everything, the way she always stood. Toren came pushing the chair, with Senna in it wrapped to the chin.

"It isn't Tuesday," said Senna, to everybody. "I'd like that known. I've been brought out on the pretext that it'll do."

And behind all of them, a little apart, with her plait down her back and her tally board left behind and nothing in her hands at all, came the girl from the weighbridge. She stopped at the edge of the first lane and looked at all of them, one after another, the way she had looked at the crew on the plate.

"Is this it?" she said.

"This is it," said Katori.

The girl looked at the three white lines, and the shed with a bucket under the drip, and the old woman in the wheeled chair, and the boy with his arm in a sling, and the big boy on the sand heap telling Otto about his heels.

"Right," she said. "I thought there'd be more."

---

The woman with the good boots came out of the shed carrying a tray.

There were three dishes on it, each covered with a square of cloth. She set it down on Dessa's table and stood behind it and looked at all of them.

"Four things," she said. "Before anybody spends anything in this yard. I'll say them once, and then I'll say them every day until you're sick of them, and then I'll say them some more." She held up a finger. "Know which is yours and which isn't." Another. "Know how much of yours there is." Another. "Take in a little that you've measured." Another. "Let it settle, and don't touch it." She put her hand down. "Then we'll talk about spending. Not before."

"That's Homura," said Rook, from the step of the shed.

"That's why I'm standing here," said the woman.

Rook said nothing to that. Jab saw his hands, on his knees, close slowly and open again.

"One," said the woman. "Which is yours. Put a hand out. Whoever's got a door."

They put their hands out: Jab, and Callie, and Toren, and the weighbridge girl, and after a moment Dessa from her table. Katori did not. Katori stood with her hands at her sides and watched, and the woman with the good boots looked at her once, for a long moment, and did not ask.

"Do the smallest thing your door does," she said. "The very smallest. And put your other hand flat on a dish."

Jab put the knife on his fist, dark. It came the way it always came, heavy in the belly, hooked at the end, the metal his mother had looked at on a gravel bar and said *well, it looks like you*. It cost nothing to hold. He had been holding it all day for four years. Then he let the light start along the edge from the grip, a hair's width of low amber, the colour of a coal with the ash still on it, and it ran along two-thirds of the blade and stuck at the belly, the way it always did, for one breath, before it caught.

He laid his other palm flat on the cloth over the first dish.

Under the cloth there was ash. He knew it before he felt it. It was cold and heavy and fine, and it did not want anything at all. It was like putting his hand on the floor of an empty house. There was nobody in it. There never had been anybody in it.

And in his other hand, in the knife, in the thin amber line along the edge, there was somebody. It was not warm, exactly. It was full. It was the difference between an empty room and a room with somebody asleep in it.

"Mine's got somebody in it," he said. "That hasn't got anybody."

"Good," said the woman, and made a mark in Dessa's book over Dessa's shoulder.

Beside him Callie had her right palm up, and a shine on it, faint, like old glass. She laid her left on the second dish. "Mine's cold," she said, "and it's mine. That's cold and it's nobody's." She thought about it. "Mine's cold like a well. That's cold like a stone in a well."

The weighbridge girl had her straight blade out, dark, and her other hand on the third dish, and her face screwed up. "It's like touching a dead fire," she said. "Like the grate in the morning. You know there was something and there isn't."

Toren had his stone out on his open palm. He was holding it the way Jab had seen him hold it at the gate on the first night, as if he were testing a stove. His other hand was on the first dish, beside Jab's. He did not say anything for a long time.

"I can't tell," he said at last.

"Try."

"I am trying. I can feel the ash. I can feel it's cold. I can't feel—" He stopped. His thumb had started to move on the stone. It stopped. "I can't feel the other. I don't know if it's there. If I look for it, I—"

"If you look for it, you're checking," said the woman. "And checking puts it out. I know. It's in Elias's book of things he says when he's tired." She did not sound impatient. "It's all right. It's the first morning. Write it, Dessa. *Toren, can't tell.* That's a result."

Dessa wrote it. Toren put his stone away in his pocket and stood with his hand on his knee, and did not look at anybody, and Jab saw what it cost him not to.

---

Then the woman swapped the dishes while they shut their eyes, and did it again, and again. Under one cloth there was ash, and under the others there was sand from the heap, and she moved them round, and they had to say which.

Jab found the ash every time. So did Callie. The weighbridge girl found it twice in three. Toren found it once, and knew he had found it by accident, and said so.

"*Toren, once, by accident, and said so,*" said the woman to Dessa. "Write the last part too."

Then Jab did something he had not meant to do.

The woman was standing behind him, between him and Callie, reaching past him to move a dish, and he felt her. He did not look for her. He felt her the way you feel somebody come into a room behind you. She was full. She was fuller than she had been on the north lip on the twelfth day, when she had put her hand on Rook's chest and come away pale; he had not been close enough then to feel anything, but he knew, now, the way you know a bucket has been refilled by the sound it makes when you set it down.

"You've got more than you had," he said, before he could stop himself. "Than on the north lip."

She went still, with her hand on the dish.

"That's rude," she said.

"Sorry."

"It's also right." She moved the dish. "Write it, Dessa. *Jab, unprompted, on a person.* And the time."

Dessa wrote it. Her pencil stopped, and she looked up at Jab over the top of the big book, and then down again.

---

"Write why," said Senna.

Everybody turned. She was sitting in her chair at the end of Dessa's table, wrapped to the chin, with her hands in her lap where she could see them.

"Why what?" said Dessa.

"Why it matters which is yours. You've written *Toren, can't tell*. You've written *Jab, on a person*. Nobody in forty years will know what any of it's for." Senna did not raise her voice. "I've been in a great many places where somebody wrote down what everybody did and nobody wrote down why. The why's always the first thing to go. You'll want it, one day. Or somebody will."

Dessa looked at the woman with the good boots.

The woman thought about it. Jab watched her think. She did it the way Rook did, seriously, as if the question deserved more than it had been given.

"Because you can borrow the one that isn't yours," she said. "Ash. Anybody's. You can take it in and spend it and it's gone and nobody's poorer. You can't borrow yourself back." She looked at Jab. "If you can't tell which is which, you spend yourself thinking it's the jar. That's how people end up on floors."

"Write that," said Senna.

Dessa wrote it.

When she had finished she turned back to the very first page of the book, which Jab had seen her write on before anybody came, and which she had covered with her hand when she saw him looking. She looked at it for a moment. Then she turned forward again to where they were, and went on.

---

The picture was at the ninth hour on the sixteenth day, and Jab asked Callie to come.

He asked her on the step of fourteen the night before, and he did not know how to ask it, so he said it badly.

"Would you count it?" he said. "Tomorrow. The knocking."

Callie looked up at him from her step.

"I always count it," she said. "I can't not."

"I know. I'd like it to be for me this time." He did not know how else to say it. "I can't hear it the way you do. I hear it and it's just noise, and I sit there and wait for the noise to stop. You hear it and you know what it's doing." He looked down the row. "I'd like somebody in that corridor to know what it's doing."

Callie was quiet for a while.

"All right," she said.

---

So she sat on the bench against the wall in the cool grey air at the bottom of the slope, with the hook across her knees, and Jab stood at the yellow line with the paper tray in his hand and his brother on the rolling bed beside him, and the grey-haired man came to the door and said, "I'll want to warm it up. Three times, maybe four."

"Four," said Tonk, from the bed. "You said four *last* time."

"I said three."

"You *did* four."

The grey-haired man looked at Tonk for a moment. Something happened in his tired face that might have been a smile, if he had had any left.

"I did four," he agreed, and went in.

The rack began to hum. Jab could not see it. He could see the edge of the machine through the door, the pale ring, and nothing else. Behind the wall the knocking started, long runs of it, fast and steady and mechanical, and he stood at the line and did not try to hear it, and let Callie hear it for him.

Her lips moved on the bench.

"Eleven," she said, low. "Eleven. Eleven." A gap. "Ten." Another run. "Eleven. Ten."

"Is that bad?"

"It's what it was on the tenth day." She kept her eyes on the door. "It's not worse. It's not better. It's the same bad."

The knocking stopped. Started. Stopped. Started. Four times, the man walked his candle along the row of windows, and Jab stood at the line and counted nothing, and Callie counted everything, out loud, low, in a flat voice like somebody reading a list of weights off a board.

Then the grey-haired man came to the door and said, "Now," and it was time.

Tonk looked at the yellow line. Then he looked at Jab.

He had the long cord in his fist, the loose end, the way he had taken it in on the tenth day. He had the wooden fish in the other hand. He held on to Jab's fingers with the cord hand, hard, for as long as it took Jab to count three, and then four, and then five.

Then he let go by himself.

"It's not metal," he said, holding up the cord. "So it can come."

"It can come," said Jab.

The door shut like a lid.

---

Doctor Keel came down the slope herself, when it was done.

She came from the room behind the desk at the top, where the picture came out, and she came down it fast, with her sleeves down for once and a sheet of something grey and shiny in her hand, and she stopped at the yellow line and looked at Jab, and Jab found that he had stopped breathing some time ago and not noticed.

"Clear," she said.

He did not understand the word.

"The bone's clear," said Doctor Keel. "I'm saying *clear*. I'm not saying *I think*. It was a picture worth the name for once; he got it on the fourth run, and it's clean. There's nothing in that bone that shouldn't be. The infection's gone out of him." She looked at Jab's face. "That's the four in five. He's in the four."

Jab sat down.

He did not decide to. He was standing at the line and then he was sitting on the concrete floor of the corridor with his back against the wall and the paper tray in his lap, and Callie was on the bench beside him, very close, not touching, and the grey-haired man was in the doorway, and Doctor Keel was standing over him with the shiny sheet, and none of it was quite real.

"Breathe," said Doctor Keel. "I'm not having you go over in my corridor. I told you that on the first day."

He breathed.

"Now the rest," she said, "because I don't do half of anything. I could see the growing plate on this one, properly, for the first time. It's damaged worse than I told you. Not gone. Worse than I'd hoped. That leg's going to be a bigger difference from the other one than I said, when he's grown. The boot's going to be higher. The operation in the middle is more likely than it was, not less." She did not look away from him. "I'd rather you had that today, in the same breath as the good one, than on a bad afternoon in three years when you're not expecting it."

"Yes," said Jab.

"The frame stays till the spring. That hasn't changed."

"No."

"And he'll start pulling." Keel turned to the rolling bed, which the nurse had brought out through the door, and on which Tonk lay flat with his eyes screwed shut and the cord in his fist, furious. "You. Can you hear me?"

"*Yes*."

"There's going to be a bar over your bed. Hanging from a frame. You'll pull yourself up on it. Every day. A little further than the day before."

Tonk opened his eyes.

"*Myself*?"

"Yourself."

"Not Jab?"

"Not Jab," said Doctor Keel. "Jab's done enough lifting. You're going to do some."

Tonk turned his head and looked at his brother, sitting on the floor of the corridor with his back against the wall.

"*Myself*," he said to him, with enormous satisfaction.

"Yourself," said Jab. His voice did not work very well. "Can you get up now?"

"No," said Doctor Keel.

"*When*?" said Tonk.

"Spring," said Doctor Keel, and went back up the slope as fast as she had come down it.

---

The grey-haired man was still in the doorway. He was looking at Callie, not at Jab.

"You counted," he said.

"Ten, four times in twenty-two runs," said Callie. "Same as the tenth day."

The man looked at her for a long moment.

"Same as the tenth day," he said. "That's the first good news I've had off that rack in a month." He rubbed the ink on his hand with his thumb. "It doesn't mean it's getting better. It means it's not getting worse this week."

"I know," said Callie.

"I'd like you to count it on the twenty-first day," he said. "When the old woman's in. If you're able."

Callie looked at Jab, sitting on the floor. Then back at the man.

"I'll be here," she said.

---

At the tenth hour the ward turned the visitors out.

Jab tied the end of the long cord to the bed rail, the way he had every night since the second, and checked the knot, and checked it again. Above Tonk's bed there was a frame now, two tall legs and a crossbar, and hanging from the crossbar on a chain there was a short smooth bar, like the rail of a gate, at the height of a small boy's reach. Tonk had pulled on it twice before he fell asleep. He had not got his shoulders off the pillow either time. He had said, both times, "*That's* further."

In the next bed, Senna lay on her back with her hands folded on the blanket and her eyes on the ceiling.

"Milo Faust," she said.

She said it to the ceiling, not loudly, in the voice of somebody stating a fact they have stated before and will state again. Jab had heard her do it last night, and the night before, at about this hour. He had not asked. It was not his.

Tonk was not asleep.

"Who's Milo?" he said, into the dark.

Senna did not answer at once. Jab stood by the bed with the knot under his fingers and did not move.

"A boy with a lamp," said Senna.

Tonk thought about this.

"Where is he?"

"He's what I say every night," said Senna. "So he's somewhere."

It was quiet for a long time in the ward. Down at the far end a nurse turned the small lamp lower. Jab thought his brother had gone to sleep.

"Can I say it?" said Tonk.

Senna turned her head on the pillow and looked across at the small shape in the next bed, under its frame, with the bar hanging over it like a rail.

"Yes," she said. "You can say it."

"Milo," said Tonk, to the ceiling, the way she had. And then, in a smaller voice, because he was four and it was dark and he had had his picture today: "Milo Faust."

"There," said Senna. "That's two of us."

Jab went out past Mrs Lask at her desk, who did not look up, and out into the frozen street under three lamps that would go out at the first hour. He walked home in the lit bits. At the loose grating between the second pole and the third he stepped wide without thinking about it.

On the step of twelve he sat down, and after a while he went down, under his breastbone, to look at the bucket. There was a hand of dark in the bottom of it, and a finger up from the bottom, scratched on the inside, a line.

*Not for anybody*, she had said.

He looked at the line for a long time. Then he came back up, and sat on his step in the cold with his hands on his knees, and did not decide anything.

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-12.md

# Chapter 12 — Toren: From the Ground Up

By the third morning the yard had a shape.

Toren could see it from the corner of the east shed, where he left the chair for Senna, out of the wind with its back to the stove pipe. He saw it the way he saw everything now, as weight, and where it went. The first lane ran along the shed wall for sixty paces, and Rook had spent two afternoons putting things in it out of the scrap bins: three posts sunk in the cinders at uneven distances, a length of old rope strung between two of them at the height of a man's hip, a timber frame at the far end made from two uprights and a crossbar and four iron brackets that did not match. Nothing in the first lane was straight, and nothing was where you expected it. Rook said that was the point.

The second lane ran to the sand heap by the berm. It was old ballast sand, grey and coarse, dumped there when the wagon lane closed and never moved, and Wyck had claimed it the way a dog claims a step. He sat on top of it most of the day with his bar across his knees and his strapped arm across his chest, and told people how to stand, and how to fall, and where they were putting their weight, and why that was stupid. There were four straw sacks at the foot of the heap and a post wrapped in old blanket. Otto spent most of his mornings there, falling over on purpose.

The third lane was the shed. It was not a lane at all, but Rook had painted a line to its door anyway, so that it would have one. Inside it was the stove and the cot and the table with the brass scale, and at the far end the bucket under the drip, going *tick*. Dessa's table stood against the wall outside the door, where the light was, with the big book open on it and a stone on each page to keep the wind off.

That was all of it. Three lines of whitewash on the cinders, some scrap, a heap of sand and a shed that leaked. Toren had been in it for two days. He had already found that he walked differently when he crossed from one lane into the next, and that he could not have said why.

---

"Two," said the woman with the good boots. "Know how much of yours there is."

She had a glass in her hand. It was small, the height of her palm, with a brass frame round it and grey sand inside, and when she turned it over the sand began to run through the waist of it in a thin grey thread.

"One turn's a minute," she said. "Near enough. I've had it since I was fourteen. It's the only thing I took off the wire that wasn't on my back."

Toren saw Rook, on the shed step, look at the glass and then look away.

"This isn't a Reading," she said. "Elias has taught some of you Readings. That's how much of the room's lit. It's a good question. This is a different one. This is how much oil's left in the lamp, today." She set the glass on Dessa's table. "Everybody does the smallest thing their door does, and holds it, from wherever you are now, down to your floor. You feel for the floor. You don't go through it. When you feel it, you stop, and I write down how many turns of the glass that took. Tomorrow we do it again. It's not a race. The numbers don't compare between doors. They compare between days."

"What's a floor?" said the weighbridge girl.

"The last place you can stop," said Jab, before the woman could answer. "And still walk home, and still do it again tomorrow."

The woman looked at him. Then she nodded, and did not say anything else about it.

---

Callie went first, because she was nearest. She stood in the first lane with her feet planted and her hands at her sides, and a shine came up over her like old glass, a skin's width off her coat, and she stood in it. Her lips moved. Toren knew she was counting. The woman watched the glass.

At thirty-eight, by Callie's lips, the shine went off her.

"Floor," said Callie.

"Thirty-eight seconds, standing," said the woman, and Dessa wrote it. "That's today."

The weighbridge girl did her blade, lit. It stood out of her fist straight and level with its frost-pale line along the edge, and she held it very still and did not look at it, and her face got whiter and whiter, and at a turn of the glass and most of another she said, "That's it, I think, is that it?" and the woman said, "You tell me," and she said, "That's it," and the blade went.

Jab lit his knife and held it. He held it through one turn of the glass, and two, and three, standing easily in the lane with the amber line lying along the edge of that fat ugly blade, and at the fifth turn he yawned.

"That'll do," said the woman. "I believe you. Put it out before you fall asleep."

"I can do it all day," said Jab. "If it's only the light."

"I know. I read your card." She turned to Toren. "You."

---

Toren took the stone out of his pocket.

He had been waiting for this since the morning before. He had lain awake in hut eleven for part of the night waiting for it, with his hand in his coat pocket on the stone and not closing on it, which was the only way he had found to keep his hand there at all.

The stone was warm. It had been warm for fifteen days, since a night on a step when he had stopped checking. It came and went under his fingers like something breathing, the least impressive thing anybody had ever felt. He did not know how full it was. He had not asked. He had been afraid, every day for fifteen days, that asking would be the same as checking, and that checking would put it out, and that this time it would stay out.

"It's not a question you can ask me," he said.

The woman waited.

"The last time it went out," said Toren, "I didn't know when it was coming back. The time before that, it was two days. I asked it, every forty steps, for two months, if it was still there. And the asking put it out." He stopped. "Rook says so. Callie says so. It's the one thing I've learned in this place that I know is true."

"Checking puts it out," said the woman. "Yes. I'm not asking you to check."

"What's the difference?"

"Checking's asking every forty steps whether it's still there," she said. "Because you're afraid it isn't. It's asking the same question over and over and not listening to the answer, because the answer isn't what you're asking for. You're asking to be told you're not going to lose it." She did not say it unkindly. "Measuring is asking once, properly, and writing the answer down, and then leaving it alone. You don't have to believe the answer. You only have to write it."

Toren looked at the glass on the table.

"One," said the woman. "The spike. Dark. Not lit. Not the other thing with your legs, and not both, and I know about both, and Dessa's told me she told you not this year. Just the spike, dark, until you feel the floor. Then stop. And don't look at the stone. Look at the glass."

---

He held the stone in his right hand, with the seam across his palm, and let the spike come.

It came the way it always came, not the way he wanted it. It came up out of his fist crooked and ugly, as long as his forearm, thick at the root with a bad heavy heel to it, grey and blunt and dark. Garrick had looked at it on the day it first came, in a stone cut with husks coming up the road, and said *keep the ugly shape, stop trying to be good at it*. Rook had looked at it for two months and not let him improve it. It sat on his fist now like a thing that had been dropped there, and he held it.

The woman turned the glass.

He did not look at the stone. He looked at the sand. It ran through the waist of the glass in a grey thread, and piled up in the bottom in a little grey hill, and the hill got taller.

Holding the spike cost something. He had never let himself notice what. He noticed it now, because he was looking for it, and it was a small steady draw from somewhere behind his breastbone, like water going out of a barrel through a pinhole. It was not frightening. It was only a thing that was happening.

The woman turned the glass again.

Somewhere in the second turn, he felt it. It was not the stone going cold. It was not a door closing. It was a thinning, the way a rope thins when you feel the strands begin to go under your hands before it parts, and he knew the way you know you have been walking too long that there was a place a little way below here where he would not be able to get up again.

"There," he said.

He let the spike go. It went back into the stone. The stone was warm in his palm. It was still warm. It was exactly as warm as it had been before he started, which was to say it was faint, and breathing, and there.

"Two turns and a quarter," said the woman. "Dark." Dessa wrote it. "That's today."

It was not much. Toren knew it was not much. Callie's thirty-eight seconds had been standing in a Ward, which cost everything, and the weighbridge girl's turn and three-quarters had been lit, and Jab had yawned at five. Two and a quarter, dark, was the smallest number anybody had said, for the cheapest thing anybody had done.

He stood in the first lane with the stone in his hand and found that he did not mind.

He had asked it how much. He had asked it once, and it had answered, and it was still there. For the first time since the Breach he had put a question to it and it had not gone out.

"Thank you," he said to the woman.

She looked at him over the glass for a moment. Then she wrote something in her own book, not Dessa's, and Toren did not see what.

"Write why," said Senna, from her chair by the stove pipe.

"Because tomorrow," said the woman, before Dessa could look at her, "you'll need to know whether today was a good day."

---

"You fall like a dropped plate," said Wyck.

He said it from the top of the sand heap, in the flat voice he used for things that were simply true. Toren was lying at the bottom of the heap on his good side, in the sand, where Wyck had told him to fall.

"I fell like you said."

"You fell like you'd been told to fall. That's different. You went down like a plate off a shelf, all at once, and you put your hand out." Wyck pointed with the bar at Toren's right hand, which was flat in the sand where it had landed. "You put your hand out and that's how people break their wrists. I know. I've done it." He did not say which time. "You fall on the road every time you do the thing with your legs. Dessa says eleven times since you started it. She counted. So you might as well learn to do it properly."

"How do you do it properly?"

"Like a sack," said Wyck. "A full sack. You don't reach for the ground. You let the ground come and you give it something round." He thought about it. "Tuck the chin. Round the back. Take it on the meat, not the points. Roll the way it wants to go. Don't put your hand down, ever, for any reason, even if your mother's at the bottom of the heap."

"And you know how to fall."

"I don't fall," said Wyck. "I've had a lot of time to watch other people do it."

Toren fell eleven more times. The last three he did it like a sack, or like something that had once seen a sack, and Wyck said "Better" once and "That'll do" once and said nothing at all the third time, which Otto told him afterward was the best thing Wyck said to anybody.

---

The first lane ended at the timber frame, and in front of the frame there was a post with a rag tied round it at the height of a man's chest.

"Twelve paces," said Rook. He was sitting on an upturned crate at the side of the lane, because he had held the Sag at first light and his hands were still doing what they did afterward. "With the struts. To the post. And stop."

"Stop?"

"Stop," said Rook. "Everybody practises going. Nobody practises stopping. You've gone past everything you ever ran at and fallen over on the other side of it." He looked at the post. "Have somewhere to be. Be there. Stay."

Toren stood at the start of the lane with his left arm strapped against his chest.

The cinders were wet. There had been frost in the night and the sun had got to it, and the whitewash lines Rook had painted had gone slick and grey where the damp had got into them. Toren looked at the line he would have to cross two paces before the post, and thought about it, and decided it did not matter.

He had somewhere to be.

The struts came. They came the way they always came, thin and uneven and bright along the outside of his shins, and the left one late, the way the left one was always late, half a breath behind the right as if it had been called from further off. There was the flat crack of them loading and a scatter of grit, and he went.

Twelve paces went by in about the time it takes to say twelve. The post came up at him. He started to stop.

His right boot came down on the whitewash line and went out from under him like a boot on ice.

He went left. There was nothing he could do about it. His weight was all going forward and to the left, and the left was where the arm was, strapped across his chest with the break in it, and he saw the cinders coming up at it and knew exactly what the noise would be. He had made it on the slabs at the bottom of the Sag on the night of the north road. His whole body knew it.

And the left strut arrived.

It was late. It was always late. It had been coming up his shin the way it always came, half a breath behind, and his foot was already gone out from under him when it got there, so it did not come up along the leg at all. It came up from the ground. It came up out of the cinders a foot to the outside of where his boot should have been, at a slant, like a prop kicked under a falling wall, and it met his leg at the knee coming the other way, and it locked.

He stopped.

He did not stop the way a person stops. He stopped the way a ladder stops against a wall. He hung there, tipped over to the left at a slant, with his right boot skidded out on the whitewash and his left leg braced into the cinders along a line of light that ran from his knee to the ground outside his foot, and his broken arm a foot off the ground, and nothing moving.

For about as long as it takes to breathe in.

Then it went out, and he dropped, and at the last moment, without thinking about it at all, he tucked his chin and rounded his back and went down onto his good shoulder like a full sack, and rolled, and lay there in the cinders on his back looking at the sky.

---

Nobody said anything.

Somewhere down at the sand heap Otto had stopped in the middle of falling over. Dessa had stopped with her pencil on the page. At the shed door, Senna had turned her chair by leaning.

"Do that again," said Wyck.

Toren lay on his back.

"Do what?" he said.

Callie was already walking over. She did not come to him. She came to the place on the lane where he had been, a pace and a half short of the post, and crouched, and looked at the ground. She did not touch it. She held the hook's handle out over it, flat, the way you hold your hand over a track to feel whether it is warm.

"Don't walk here," she said, to nobody. "Anybody. Don't walk here."

Rook had got up off his crate. He came down the lane slowly, with one hand out to nothing, and stopped over Toren and looked down at him.

"What did you do?" he said.

"I fell."

"No," said Rook. "You nearly fell."

"That wasn't Ward," said the woman with the good boots, from the shed door.

"No," said Rook. He had not taken his eyes off Toren. "It's not his door. That was the struts. That was his hardware." He said the word as if he had never said it about a door before and was trying the taste of it. "That was his hardware doing something I've never seen it do."

"That's a guard," said Wyck.

They looked at him. He was on top of the sand heap with his bar across his knees.

"When you stand to take a hit," said Wyck. "Arms up, weight down. That's your guard. That's what the men at home called it, when they fought on a pay night. You put your guard up." He pointed with the bar. "That was his legs putting a guard up. That was a strut stood there to take the ground instead of pushing off it."

Dessa had her pencil on the page.

"What do I write?" she said.

"*Guard*," said Wyck.

"*Stride, held*," said Rook.

"Write what he did," said Senna from her chair, "not what you think it was. You'll only have to cross it out. Write: *the strut stood up.*"

Dessa looked at the three of them. Then she bent over the book and wrote for some time. When Toren got up and came and looked, later, she had written all three, one under the other, with a line drawn down the side to join them, and at the bottom, in smaller letters, *Toren fell like a sack after. Wyck's lane.*

---

Rook would not let him do it again that day.

"You'll try to," he said. "You'll try to do the thing you just did, and you'll try to do it on purpose, and it'll put you on that arm. I'm not having you on that arm. Tomorrow."

"Tomorrow what?"

"Tomorrow we'll see if it'll come when you ask," said Rook, "and I'll have Wyck build you something to ask it with that won't break anything. And her in the shed will want to measure you first. Sit down."

Toren sat down on Rook's crate. His hands were shaking. He looked at them for a while. It was the first time they had shaken like Rook's.

At the end of the lane, a pace and a half short of the post, Callie was still crouched with the hook's handle held out flat over the cinders, looking at the ground as if it were a page she had not finished reading.

---

Wyck built it overnight.

He could only use one arm, so Otto did the lifting and Wyck did the telling. By first light there was a rope hanging from the crossbar of Rook's timber frame at the end of the first lane, and on the end of the rope a straw sack stuffed so full it was nearly round, hanging at the height of a boy's knee. You pulled it back along the lane on a second rope and let it go, and it swung down and through, and hit whatever was standing under the crossbar.

"Knee height," said Wyck. "Not higher. It's not to hit him. It's to hit what he puts in front of him." He looked at Toren. "Keep the arm in. You put that arm anywhere near the sack and I'll take it off you myself, and I've only got one hand to do it with."

"Nobody pulls it back further than that mark," said the woman with the good boots. She had painted a line on the cinders herself, short, in whitewash, six paces out. "That's as hard as it hits. That's today."

"You measure everything," said the weighbridge girl.

"I do."

"Even how hard you hit somebody with a sack."

"Especially that," said the woman.

Otto pulled the sack back to the line. Toren stood under the crossbar with his feet set and his arm strapped against his chest.

"When you're ready," said Rook.

---

The first time, he built the struts the way he always built them.

They came up the outside of his shins, the right and then the left, late, and he felt them load the way they loaded before a run, a coiled feeling like a held breath, and he held them there and tried not to let them go. He tried to keep them the way they had been in the fall. Rigid. Held.

"Now," said Rook, and Otto let the sack go.

It came down the lane with a noise like wind. Toren watched it come and held the struts, and at the last instant, as the sack arrived, they did what they had been built to do.

They fired.

He went sideways out from under the crossbar like a stone out of a sling, and the sack went through the place where he had been, and he landed in the cinders two paces off to the right, and rolled, like a sack, and ended up sitting.

There was a small silence.

Then the weighbridge girl laughed. It came out of her all at once, high and startled, and she clapped both hands over her mouth and went scarlet and said through her fingers, "Sorry. I'm sorry. You went *so far*."

Toren sat in the cinders and looked at the place he had been.

He found that he was laughing too. It was not much of a laugh. It was the first one that had come out of him in Meridian that was not a sound in place of something else. He sat there with his arm strapped to his chest and laughed at the place he had been, and at the sack swinging back and forth through it, and at himself, and nobody in the yard told him to stop.

"You loaded them," said Rook, when he had finished. "You built them to go, and then you asked them not to."

"I know."

"Again."

---

The second time, he built them rigid from the start. He did not let them coil. He made them straight and hard and held them the way you hold a bar across a door, square under his body, and his knees locked with them, and his hips, and everything he had.

Otto let the sack go.

It hit. It hit the struts dead square at the knee, and the struts held, for about as long as it takes to blink, and all the weight of that swinging sack went straight through them into his legs, and his legs went straight into his hips, and his hips went nowhere, because he had locked them, and so the whole of it went up through his spine and into the strapped arm and came out of him as a noise he had not made since the slabs.

He was on his back. He did not remember going there.

The arm. It was the arm; something in it had jarred against the splint, deep, along the line he could have drawn on paper, and the dull ache there had become a bright one. He lay in the cinders and held it against his chest with his good hand and breathed through his teeth.

The woman with the good boots was kneeling beside him. He had not seen her come.

"Where?"

"The arm," said Toren. "It jarred. Inside the splint. It's—it's not like the first night. It's not as bad as that. But it's there."

She looked at him for about four seconds without touching him. Then she put two fingers along the splint, lightly, from the wrist to the elbow, and watched his face while she did it.

"That's today," she said.

"I can go again."

"You can. You won't." She sat back on her heels. "You told me where it hurt and how much. That's the most useful thing anybody's done in this lane all morning. If you'd lied about it, I'd have let you go again, and you'd have been on your back in Keel's room this afternoon with that splint off." She stood up. "Sit on the heap with Wyck. The two of you can watch other people do things."

Rook, on his crate, said, "Good."

Toren looked at him.

"You said it," said Rook. "Out loud. Where it hurt." He looked at the sack, still swinging on its rope, slower and slower. "It's harder than taking the sack."

---

So he sat on the sand heap with Wyck for the rest of the morning, two boys with one good arm between them, and watched other people do things.

He watched Callie stand in her Ward in the first lane, and lift one foot, very slowly, and watched the shine go off her like water off a tipped plate. He watched the weighbridge girl cut a straw sack in half with her straight blade, clean, in one stroke, and then stand looking at the two halves on the cinders as if she had not known she could do it. He watched Jab light his knife and let it go dark and light it again, over and over, a dozen times, faster each time. He watched Dessa write all of it.

"It held for you," said Wyck, after a long time. "When you weren't trying."

"Yes."

"Same as mine." Wyck turned the bar over in his left hand. He did not drop it. "Rook says I'm a person who decided, over and over, for years, not to be moved. I never decided anything. I just didn't get moved. And then one day I found out it was a thing I did." He looked at the sack. "When I try to do it, it's worse. When I lean on it to see if it's there, it moves my collarbone."

"So what do you do?"

"I don't know yet," said Wyck. "I'm finding out. Keel says six weeks." He was quiet for a while. "You locked your knees. The second time. I watched. You locked the struts and then you locked yourself behind them, all the way up, like a post. A post takes the hit into the ground if it's in the ground. You weren't in the ground. So it went into you." He pointed with the bar at Toren's legs. "Lock the struts. Not you. You stay soft behind them. Like a man behind a door, not a man who is one."

Toren thought about that.

"That's not all of it," he said.

"No," said Wyck. "It's my bit. Somebody else'll have the rest."

---

Somebody else had the rest.

He found it at the end of the day, when the yard had emptied and the light had gone long and orange across the cinders from the west. Callie was at the end of the first lane, by the post, crouched where she had crouched the morning before. She had not been anywhere near him all afternoon. He had seen her look over at the heap once, and then back at the ground.

She had drawn on the cinders. She had done it with the handle of the hook, the end of the conduit, pressing it in to make marks, and there were three of them in a row, each about the size of a boot, with lines scratched round them.

"Come and look," she said, without looking up. "Don't step on them."

He came and crouched beside her, carefully, with his arm against his chest.

"That's yesterday," she said, and pointed at the first. "When you fell. That's your right boot, skidded, there, on the whitewash. And that—" she pointed a hand's width out to the left, at a mark he would not have seen if she had not drawn round it "—is where the strut came down. Look at it. It's deep at the outside edge. It's on a slant. It's a foot outside where your boot should've been."

Toren looked. He could see it, now she had drawn round it: a narrow slot pressed into the cinders at an angle, like the mark a pick leaves.

"That's this morning, the first time." She moved the hook's handle to the second drawing. "Your strut marks are along your heels. Right under them. And they're scuffed backward, look, spraying the cinders out behind, like a horse that's pushed off. That's them firing." She moved to the third. "And that's the second time. Square under your boot. Pressed straight down. Flat. Like a post driven in."

She sat back on her heels and looked at all three of them together.

"The first one came up from the ground," she said. "Yours go down from your knee. They always go down from your knee. That's where you build them from. You build them from the knee down, along your leg, to push off the ground. The one that held didn't come from your knee. It came from out here." She put the handle on the slanted mark. "It came up from the ground and met your leg coming the other way."

Toren looked at the mark for a long time.

"Because it was late," he said.

Callie turned her head.

"The left one's always late," he said. "It always has been. Since the first day Rook taught me, in the cart. Half a breath behind the right. Yesterday my foot had already gone out from under me when it got there, so it couldn't come up my leg. There wasn't a leg where it was going. So it came up out of the ground where my foot should've been." He heard himself laughing again, very quietly. "It held because it was late."

"From the ground up," said Callie.

---

He stood up.

He did it slowly, and stood with his feet set, on the cinders beside her three drawings, with no sack and no frame and nobody watching but her.

He did not build the struts. He did not let them come up his shins the way they always came. He thought about the left one, the late one, and he thought about where it had been going when his foot went out from under him, and instead of calling it up from the knee he put it where the drawing was. He put the foot of it first, down in the cinders a foot outside his own boot, at a slant, and then he let it come up from there, toward him, the way it had come.

It came.

It was thin and uneven and bright, and it came up out of the ground at a slant like a prop under a wall, and it met his knee, and for about as long as it takes to breathe in, it held. There was nothing pushing on it. It was only there, standing, the way a strut stands under a roof.

Then it went.

He stood on the cinders. He did not fall over.

"That's the late one," said Callie. "That's the one that holds."

"It went out."

"It came, though." She stood up too, with one hand on her ribs. "You asked it and it came. It didn't come yesterday when you asked it. It only came when you fell." She looked down at the drawings. "Now you know where to ask it from."

Toren looked at her. She was looking at the ground.

"Wyck said lock the struts and not myself," he said. "Stay soft behind them."

"Then that's two things," said Callie. "From the ground up. And soft behind." She put the hook over her shoulder. "Tomorrow you'll have three. Everybody always wants you to have three."

---

Jab was on the step of twelve when he got back to the row, with the fish-knife on his fist, dark, turning it slowly to look at it in the last of the light.

Toren sat down on the step of eleven. The stone in his pocket was warm.

"How do you light it?" he said.

Jab looked at him.

"Your knife," said Toren. "How do you light it? Not the metal. The light." He looked at his own right hand. "Mine comes when it likes. It's come twice when I needed it, lit so hard it was like carrying a lamp. The rest of the time I don't know how to ask. I tried in the lane today. I got a flicker and it went."

Jab was quiet for a while.

"I start at the grip," he said. "It comes out along the edge from the grip end. Like a line of wet going along a cloth. It goes clean for most of the way, and then it gets to the belly, where it's fat, and it sticks. Every time. For one breath. Then it catches and goes on to the hook." He turned the knife. "I've been doing it four years. It's always stuck at the belly. I don't know why."

"Show me."

Jab showed him. The amber line came out of the grip end of the blade, thin as a hair, the colour of a coal with the ash still on it, and ran along the edge, and at the swell of the belly it stopped, and guttered, and hung there for one breath, and caught, and went on round to the hook.

"Now you," said Jab.

---

Toren let the spike come.

It came crooked and ugly, the way it always came, as long as his forearm, with its bad thick heel. He looked at it beside Jab's knife in the dusk. Jab's knife was ugly too, as ugly as a door hinge, but it was a shape. It was a shape somebody had meant. Everything about it, the heavy spine and the fat belly and the little hook, was for something. Toren's spike was not for anything. It was a thing that had come out of his fist on the second day of his life with it because a husk was coming, and had never changed since.

He tried to make it a little better.

He did not decide to. He was looking at Jab's knife, and at his own spike, and something in him leaned toward the one from the other, the way you straighten a picture on a wall without thinking, and he tried to take the heel off the spike and make the line of it cleaner, a little more like a thing somebody had meant.

The spike guttered like a lamp in a draught and went out.

He sat on the step with his fist empty.

"Don't make it nice," said Jab.

Toren looked at him.

"I watched you do it," said Jab. "You looked at mine and you tried to make yours like it. It went." He put his own knife out, and it went back into the metal on his neck cord, and he sat with his hands on his knees. "When I was thirteen, on a gravel bar, with sixty people watching, I pulled this out of a river stone. It was the ugliest thing anybody in the valley had ever seen come out of a Kindling. My father's was long and straight and narrow, like a reed. Mine was a fish knife. The kind every house has two of." He was quiet for a moment. "My mother came up behind my father with my brother on her back, and she looked at it, and she said, *Well, it looks like you.*"

Toren said nothing.

"*That's the whole of it*, she said. *Don't spend too long.*" Jab looked down the row at the lamp, which was not lit yet. "They're both dead. The bridge went. I never asked her what she meant by the last bit. I thought I had years."

---

The lamp at the end of the row came on, flat and white.

"My father said *keep the ugly shape*," said Toren. "*Stop trying to be good at it.* In a stone cut, with husks coming up the road. It was the last thing he ever told me about it."

Jab turned his head and looked at him.

They sat on their two steps, one door apart, and neither of them said anything for a long time. Toren thought about a woman on a gravel bar he would never see, with a baby on her back, looking at a fish knife, and a man in a stone cut with husks coming, looking at a spike. Two people who had never met, four hundred miles apart, saying the same thing to two boys about the shape of what came out of them. He did not know what it meant. He thought it meant something.

"Light it," said Jab. "The ugly one. The one that looks like you."

Toren let the spike come.

It came crooked and ugly and heavy in the heel, and this time he did not look at Jab's knife. He looked at his own. He let it be what it was. And then he asked it for the light, not the way he would ask a door to open but the way you would ask somebody sitting next to you on a step to pass you something, without looking, because you knew they were there.

The light came.

It came along the edge of the spike from the root, thin and hard and white-grey, a cold line lying along the inner curve of the crooked blade. It came all at once and it shook. It shook the way his hands had shaken on Rook's crate. It flickered at the heel and at the point and along the middle, and it would not settle, and he held it and it shook.

"You're holding it like somebody's going to take it off you," said Jab.

Toren did not answer.

He thought about a grey coat's glove at the north gate, held out for the stone. About a paper with nineteen days on it, and his father's hand on his shoulder. About a greenish light no brighter than a jar of fireflies, held up on a channel wall, that did not go out. About everything that had been taken off him since the spring, one thing after another, until he had stopped being able to hold anything without waiting for the hand that would come for it.

Nobody was going to take this. It was his. It was the ugly one. It looked like him.

He let go of it a little. Not all the way. A little, the way you ease your grip on a rope you know will hold.

The light steadied. It lay along the crooked edge of the spike, thin and white-grey and cold, and it did not shake, for one breath, and another, and a third.

Then it flickered, and he let it go before it could go out on its own, and the spike went back into the stone, and the stone was warm.

"Three," said Jab.

"Three," said Toren.

---

"You look at the belly," he said, after a while.

Jab turned his head.

"When you light yours. I watched your eyes. You start at the grip, and your eyes go along with the light, and when it gets to the belly your eyes stop there. Because that's the part that cuts. That's the part you've used for four years, on the wood, on the hound." Toren nodded at Jab's fist. "The light stops where you're looking. Look at the hook."

Jab was silent for a long moment.

Then he let the knife come again, dark, on his fist, and started the light at the grip, and Toren watched him fix his eyes on the little hook at the end of the blade and hold them there, and not let them go to the belly at all.

The amber line came out of the grip. It ran along the edge. It came to the swell of the belly, and it did not stop. It went through the belly like water going through a gap in a wall, with a flicker in it, half a breath, less, and on round to the hook, and the whole edge was lit.

Jab stared at it.

"Four years," he said.

"You never had anybody watching your eyes," said Toren.

Jab sat on his step with the whole edge of his knife lit in the dusk, amber and even from the grip to the hook, and looked at it. Then he looked at Toren. Toren had seen his face do a great many things in seventeen days, most of them in corridors, and he had not seen it do this.

"Don't tell Dee," said Jab. "She'll want it measured."

"She'll want it measured," Toren agreed.

They sat on their two steps in the light of the lamp, and neither of them went in for a long time.

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-13.md

# Chapter 13 — Callie: Three Answers

It rained in the night, hard, the way it rained on the plain in the spring, and by first light the bottom of the Sag was a lake.

Callie saw it from the south lip before anybody told her. The black water that usually stood in the joints between the slabs had come up and joined, and lay across the whole of the bottom of the dip in one sheet, flat and brown and still, with the tops of the tilted slabs sticking out of it like the backs of drowned cattle. The east ditch was full to the lip. The white post with the number six on it stood in water to its knees. There was a mist on it, low and grey, not the thick white fog of the mornings before but a thin one, the kind that lifts in rags.

"The pipe's blocked," said Pell. He had the atlas open against his chest to keep the rain off it. "The man at the gate said. It's jammed at the east end. It does it after a big rain, and the water backs up the ditch, and then the slabs shift." He looked at the post. "He said it moved another of them last year. Before anybody got down to it."
