She came up. She had her left wrist against her chest and her face was the colour of the fog, and she said, very clearly, in the voice of somebody who wants it known, "I fell right. I fell right, he'll want to know, I fell *right*—"

"I know," said Toren. "I saw. Go."

---

They came out of the white at the foot of the west shoulder.

The fog was thinner there, going up the slope, and he could see the gap in the shoulder above them, the long easy slope that came down from the rim to the ditch, the one door. Jab was in it.

Jab had lit his knife.

The amber line lay along the whole edge of it, from the grip to the hook, without a gutter at the belly, and Jab had stepped out of the door onto the open slope with the knife held up high in front of him like a lantern, and the husks that had come across the bottom behind them, following them through the fog, were going to him.

Not to them. To him.

Toren understood it all at once, the way Jab had once understood a fence. Husks came at anything worked. Anything lit. A light held up in the fog was the brightest worked thing on the whole Sag, and every husk in the bottom of the dip turned to it like water turning to a drain. Jab had not lit it to fight them. He had lit it to call them. He was standing out on the open slope with the whole of his light held up, calling them off three people coming out of the fog behind him.

A husk reached him. He put the knife through it, belly first, the fat of the blade, and it came apart. Another. On the rim above him Ines stood up off her stone and loosed, and the arrow went into the fog, and something in the fog stopped moving.

"Go up," said Toren to the weighbridge girl. "Up the door. Along the rim, to the lip. Dessa's there. She'll count you."

The girl went. She went up the long slope past Jab with her wrist against her chest, and Jab moved as she passed him, half a step, so that his light was between her and the bottom, and did not look at her.

---

Callie had not gone up.

She was standing at the foot of the door, at the bottom of the slope, beside the stone wing wall where the west mouth of the pipe came out under the road. She was not looking at Jab, or at the husks, or at Toren. She was looking at the pipe.

"The water's stopped," she said.

Toren looked. The west mouth of the pipe was a stone arch as wide as a man's shoulders, set in the wing wall, with a fringe of dead grass hanging over it. Since the rain it had been running: a steady low pour of black water out of the arch and into the ditch, day and night, loud enough to hear.

It was not running. There was water in the bottom of the arch, standing, black and still. Nothing coming out.

"Something's in it," said Callie. "Something big enough to stop it." She turned her head and looked up the slope, at Jab's lit knife, and then east, across the fog, toward the ditch on the other side, where the husks had come up and where the pipe began. "They didn't come at the light."

"What didn't?"

"Whatever came up after the husks," said Callie. "It didn't come to the light. It went to the dark. And the only dark there is between there and here is—"

She did not finish. She was already moving. She went along the foot of the wing wall to the arch with the hook in both hands, bolt in, the long reach, and Toren saw what she was going to do a heartbeat before she did it, and saw what it would leave her with, and could not stop her, and would not have.

She put the curve of the hook across the mouth of the pipe, corner to corner, the way the fence rail had lain across the other end five days ago. She planted her feet in the ditch. She did not grip.

The hook went pale.

---

Something hit it from inside.

Toren heard it. It was a sound like a sack of stones thrown against a gate. The hook did not move. The whole arch of the pipe seemed to ring with it, and a spray of black water came out round the steel, and in the dark behind the pale curve something long and low and the colour of wet ash drew its head back, and a second shape moved behind it, and a third.

Hounds. In the pipe. Where the husks had not gone.

"*Ines!*" Toren shouted. "*Hounds! In the pipe!*"

On the rim above, Ines turned. He saw her look down at the arch, and at Callie standing in front of it with her feet in the ditch and her hands on a pale hook, and he saw her understand. She did not draw. There was nothing to draw at; they were in the dark behind the steel. She knelt on the rim with an arrow on the string, and waited, and Toren knew she was counting.

Callie was counting too. Out loud, low, flat.

"Nine. Ten. Eleven."

She had nothing on her. The Ward was in the hook. There was no shine on her at all, not a skin's width, and she stood with her feet in the black water at the mouth of a pipe with three hounds on the other side of a hook, in her coat, with nothing between her and anything.

Toren went down the slope to her.

He did not decide to. He was at the top of it and then he was beside her at the bottom, on the wing-wall side, with the spike on his fist, and he asked it for the light.

He asked it the way Jab had told him. Not like a door. Like somebody sitting beside you on a step.

The light came. Thin and hard and white-grey, along the crooked edge, and it shook, and he did not hold it like somebody was going to take it off him, and it steadied. He stood beside Callie with the light held low across the mouth of the arch, beside her hook, where the hounds could see it.

*Light where you don't want them*, Jab had shouted on the north road. *They come at the dark bit.*

Inside the pipe, the long low shape drew back from the light. He heard it go, scrabbling, back into the dark.

"Twenty," said Callie. "Twenty-one."

---

"*Toren.*"

It was Jab. From the door, above them. Toren looked up.

Jab was standing at the top of the slope in the gap in the shoulder with his knife lit, and the husks that had come to his light were down, most of them; there was grey lying all over the slope below him in long ropes, and Ines had put two arrows into the fog and had one left on the string. Jab was still there. He was standing in the one door with the whole edge of his knife burning.

But he was not looking at the husks. He was not looking at Toren, or at Callie, or at the pipe.

He was looking east. Across the fog. At the bottom of the Sag, where the line was, where there was nothing to see but white.

His face was wrong. Toren had seen it the other way round on the steps: all his attention put down on a table and his hands taken off it. This was the opposite. This was the face of somebody whose attention has been picked up off the table by somebody else and carried away, and who is following it.

"Come up," said Jab. "Here. The door."

Toren looked at Callie.

"Go," said Callie, without looking at him. "Twenty-nine. Thirty. I've got it. It's in the hook. Go."

"You've got nothing on you."

"I know what I've got on me. Thirty-two. Go."

Toren went up the slope.

---

"Stand here," said Jab.

He moved out of the gap in the shoulder, and put Toren in it, with his hands. He did it the way he had put his brother in the angle of a wall on the north road, Toren thought afterward: quickly, without asking, and exactly.

"Light where you don't want them," said Jab. "The grey ones come at it. They'll all come at it. Let them come at you. Hold it." He looked down at the spike on Toren's fist, lit, white-grey, steady. "That's good. Hold it like that."

"Where are you going?"

Jab was already looking east again. Into the fog. His knife had gone dark on his fist; Toren had not seen him put it out.

"She's worse," said Jab.

"*Who?*"

Jab did not answer. He did not know how, Toren thought afterward; or he knew and there was not time. He went down off the shoulder into the fog on the far side of the door, dark, with no light on him at all, and the white closed over him and he was gone.

Toren stood in the one door with the spike lit on his fist.

He knew who. He had seen her go. He had seen it at the very start, when the ground spoke and the fog sat up and a woman's voice at the south lip shouted *weight*. He had seen a grey shape at the south lip leave its hollow for the first time in two years and go down, alone, into the white, toward the corner where the slab had gone, to lay back down a line that had folded. Nobody else had been looking at her. Nobody ever was.

A husk came up the slope out of the fog at his light.

He put the spike through it. The light shook. He did not let it. He held it like a thing that was his, that looked like him, that nobody was going to take.

Another. Another. His arm was in its sling and his right hand was doing all of it, and the seam across the palm was on fire, and the third one got past the point and hit him at the knee, and he went down.

He went down like a sack. Chin tucked. Back round. Onto the meat of his good shoulder, not the arm, never the arm, and rolled, and came up on one knee in the gap in the shoulder with frost and ash all down one side of him.

The spike was still lit.

He looked at it. He had fallen, and it had not gone out. It had never not gone out. It lay along the crooked edge, thin and white-grey and cold and steady, as if falling were a thing that happened to other people.

He got up. He stood in the one door, where Jab had put him, with the light held out where he did not want them. Below him, at the foot of the slope, a girl with nothing on her stood with her feet in a ditch and her hands on a pale hook across the mouth of a pipe, counting out loud.

"Forty-one," said Callie. "Forty-two."

Beyond her, the fog lay across the bottom of the Sag, white and flat and silent, and somewhere in it, going east without a light, was Jab.

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-15.md

# Chapter 15 — Jab: Below the Line

It had started four minutes before he gave Toren the door.

He had been standing out on the open slope below the gap in the shoulder with the whole edge of the knife lit and held up high, calling the grey ones off three people coming out of the fog, and they had come to it the way water comes to a drain. He had been busy. He had been putting the belly of the blade through husks one after another, the fat part, the part for when you can't get behind a thing, and watching Ines's arrows go past him into the white, and keeping his light between the bottom of the Sag and a girl going up the slope behind him with her wrist held against her chest.

And under all of it, under his breastbone, a finger's width behind, the floor had tipped.

He had felt it tip the way you feel a boat take a wave from a side you were not watching. Not far, at first. A lean. The old lean, the one that woke him at the second hour, the one that had only ever said one thing about one person. It said the same thing now.

*Worse.*

It did not say it about Tonk. Tonk was six hundred yards behind him and a berm and a wall away, in the fifth bed on the right, under a frame with a bar hanging over it on a chain, and Jab knew exactly where that was the way you know where your own hand is in the dark. This was not behind him. It was in front of him. It was out there in the fog, east, across the bottom of the Sag, where there was nothing to see but white and nobody he knew.

It had never done that. It had never leaned toward somebody he could not see. It had never leaned toward anybody who was not his brother, except once, in a shed, with his eyes shut and a woman with a crooked wrist putting his hand where she wanted it.

He had not asked it. He had a rule about that. He had been too busy with the grey ones to ask it anything at all, and it had come on its own, the way it came at the second hour, and it was not wrong in the direction he wanted, because he did not want anything from the fog. He did not know who was in it.

*I don't know yet whether they're one thing or two*, she had said, in the shed, with the bucket going *tick* at the far end. *I'll know when I've seen both do something I didn't set up.*

Nobody had set this up.

---

He went down off the shoulder on the far side of the door, dark.

He had put the light out before he went. He did not remember doing it. The grey ones came at light. The whole of the bottom of the Sag was full of fog and there were grey ones in it still, he could hear them, and a boy going across it with a knife lit up like a lantern would have every one of them on him before he had gone twenty paces. So he went dark, with the metal on his fist heavy and blunt and hooked at the end, the shape the light did not change, a tool for splitting fish.

The fog closed over him.

It was cold. It was colder than the air above it, and wet, and it smelled of stirred ditch and old stone and something else he did not have a word for, sharp at the back of the nose, like the air after lightning. He could see his own hand in front of him and not much past it. Under his boots the slabs of the bottom of the Sag tilted one way and then another, with black water standing in the joints, and he went across them the way he had gone across the flooded creek on the road, a long time ago: slowly, from the bottom up, putting each foot down and waiting for the stone to tell him what it was going to do before he put his weight on it.

The lean pulled him. It did not pull like a hand. It pulled like the slope of a floor, so that he found he was walking a little to the left of where he had meant to, and then a little more, and he let it.

Something came at him out of the white.

He did not see it. He heard it, the scrabble of too many legs on wet stone, low and to his right, and he turned toward the sound with the knife coming up, and lit it. Not the edge. Not the belly. He lit the hook, the last inch at the end of the blade, curled like a bent finger, the way he had lit it for the rail at the jam, and he put the lit hook into the grey shape where its shoulder would be as it came out of the fog at him, and drew it through, and put the light out again before the ash had finished falling.

One breath of light. He counted it. It had cost him the price of one breath of light, which was nearly nothing, and he had the rest.

He went on.

---

There was something lying across the bottom of the Sag that he could feel.

He had not known he would be able to. Callie said it was like a clean sheet of paper laid over the ground, so that where it lay she could not read anything. Dessa said it was like somebody holding their breath in a room. To Jab, going across it in the fog with the lean pulling him left, it was neither. It was like walking across the floor of a house where somebody upstairs was holding a door shut against a wind, and you could feel it through the whole house, in the boards, a strain, a leaning, the way you feel a big animal's breathing through a wall.

It was held. Something was holding it. And the holding came from in front of him, from the east, from exactly the place the lean was pulling him toward.

He came to the place where the ground went down.

It went down all at once, in front of his boots: the edge of a slab, tilted, and beyond it the next slab a foot lower, gone down into black water at a slant like a door off its hinges. There was bright dirt along the broken edge where it had been hidden a long time and was not hidden now. There was a white post standing up out of the water beside it, at a lean, with an iron cap.

Six.

He went down onto the dropped slab. The water came up to his shins. It was very cold.

And there she was.

---

She was standing at the far corner of the dropped slab, in the fog, where the east ditch came in. She was standing the way she always stood, the way he had seen her stand every morning for two weeks from the one door, the one grey shape at the south lip that nobody watched: feet planted, arms out from her sides.

Except that one arm was not out. Her left hand was pressed flat against her side, under her ribs, and held there. Only her right arm was out. She was holding the line with one hand.

Her face was the colour of the fog.

She turned her head and saw him. He saw her see him, and see what he was, a boy of seventeen with a dark knife on his fist, knee-deep in a ditch in the bottom of her Sag.

"Get back to your post, boy," she said.

Her voice was very low and very rough, like a gate that wanted oil, and it did not shake. Nothing about her shook. That was the thing he would remember. She stood in the black water with one hand out, holding a road shut, and her face was grey, and there was a sweat on it like a sheen of wet on a stone, and nothing about her shook at all.

He put his hand on her arm. The one pressed to her side.

The floor under his breastbone went over.

It did not tip. It went. It went the way it had gone at the culvert on the north road with his brother going grey under his hand, all at once and all the way, so that he had to put his other hand out against her shoulder to stay standing, and it did not say *worse*. It did not say anything. It pointed. It pointed under his hand, under her hand, under the ribs on the left side, into the dark inside her, where something that should have been whole was not whole, and where her blood was going, a great deal of it, a great deal too fast, somewhere it should not go.

"You're bleeding," he said.

"I'm holding."

"Inside. Here." He did not take his hand away. "You're bleeding inside."

"Something came up at the corner," said the day holder, "when it folded. It hit me in the side. I went down." She did not look at him. She looked at the fog in front of her where the line was. "I got up. I laid it back down. That's what you do." Her mouth moved on something that was not quite a word. "Then I'll bleed and hold."

"You'll bleed out holding."

She did not answer.

He watched her not answer. He watched her stand there with her one arm out, and her feet in the black water in the corner of a dropped slab, a woman of fifty-six who had held this road alone for two years while crews came and went and nobody watched her, and he thought: *nobody saw.* She had been hit in the fog, and gone down, and got up, and laid a folded line back down over the whole bottom of the Sag with a hand pressed to her side, and nobody had seen any of it. Nobody had come.

"I've got two years of this road," said the day holder. "I know what it costs."

Her knees went.

---

He caught her. He did not catch her well. She was heavier than she looked and he had the knife on one fist, and he let the knife go back into the metal on his neck cord and got both arms under her and went down with her onto the dropped slab, into the water, and got her head up out of it against his knee.

The line went.

He felt it go. It went the way a held breath goes, all at once. The strain in the floor of the house was gone. The ground under his knees, which had been the ground under something, was only ground again, wet slab and black water and silt, and in the joints of the slabs around him something shifted, down in the dark under the stone, the way a thing shifts when a lid comes off it.

He had time to think: *it's open.*

And then something else came down.

It came from the north, from the far lip, from a long way off, and he felt it arrive the way you feel a second pair of hands take the other end of a heavy thing you have just dropped. It came down over the whole bottom of the Sag at once, over him and her and the dropped slab and the black water, and it was not the same as hers. Hers had been a door held shut. This was a road shut. He knew it. He had seen it done on the north road on the night he came in, from the angle of a wall, with his brother against his chest: a man with his feet planted and his hands out from his sides, and the road on either side of him gone wrong.

Rook had taken the whole of it.

The shifting in the joints stopped. The ground under Jab's knees was the ground under something again.

He did not have time to think about what that cost. He had a woman's head on his knee and her blood going where it should not, and her face was not the colour of the fog any more. It was going past that.

---

He put his hand flat on her chest.

Not on her side. Not where the bleeding was; he did not know anything about where the bleeding was, and he had a rule about that, written on a card, pressed hard with the pencil. On her chest, over the breastbone, the way he had at the culvert, the way he had on the tenth day in the ward with the pin going red. Over the heart.

The heart was going very fast and very small, like a bird's.

He did not push. He had learned that much on a road and it had cost him an hour of a bad night and the use of his hands. He did not reach, or lean on it, or ask it to run uphill.

And he could not stop wanting.

He tried. He had tried in the shed with a stranger's cold hand in his, and he had not been able to, and he could not now. He wanted this woman to live more than he had wanted anything since the culvert. He did not know her name. He had never spoken to her. He had watched her every morning for two weeks, the one shape at the south lip, and he had not once thought about whether anybody else was watching her, and now he knelt in a ditch with her head on his knee and wanted her to live so much it was like a stone in his chest.

It went out of him anyway.

It went out of him without asking his permission, the way it had at the culvert. There was no decision in it anywhere. It came up out of the place it lived and went forward out of his chest, down his arm, through his palm, into the chest under his hand, and it was not a blade and it had no edge and it was not any shape at all.

It was warmth. It was the plainest thing in the world.

---

Her heart slowed.

He felt it slow. The bird under his hand went from a flutter to a beat, and from a small beat to a bigger one, and settled, the way a horse settles when you put your hand on its neck in a bad stall. Her breathing, which had been quick and high and shallow, went down into her. It went deep, and ragged, and regular.

She shook. It came up through her from the shoulders to the heels, hard, and again, and her teeth knocked together, and he thought of the Elder in her kitchen with the lamp lit, four hundred miles north, telling him what to watch for on the road. *Shaking is the body fighting. Be glad when it shakes.*

He was glad.

Her colour did not come back. He knew it would not. He knew, the way he had known at the culvert, that whatever was broken inside her was still broken, and whatever was bleeding was still bleeding. He was not mending anything. He was not even touching it. He was holding her up. He was keeping the whole of her going, the heart and the breath and the warmth of her, while the part of her that was broken went on being broken, so that there would still be a her for somebody to mend.

That was all it was. He did not know how long it could be.

He counted.

He counted in breaths, because there was nothing else to count in, and every sixteen of hers he called a minute, because the Elder had taught him that a sleeping grown woman breathes about sixteen times in a minute, and he had checked it on the road against Tonk's twenty-four and his own. It was not exact. It was his.

One minute. Two.

The bucket was going down.

---

He could feel it go.

He had never felt it go before. At the culvert it had simply gone out, like a lamp, and he had found himself sitting down in the ash. In the snow he had not known anything until his hand would not close. This time he knew. This time he had been down to look at it every night for eleven nights, since a woman in a shed told him to, and he knew what it looked like: a hand of dark in the bottom of a bucket, and a finger up from the bottom, scratched on the inside, a line.

It was going down the way water goes down in a bucket with a hole in it, steadily, a little every breath.

Four minutes. Five.

He thought about the line.

*That's your floor*, she had said. *You never go below it. Not for anybody.*

*Especially not for anybody.*

He had not said yes. She had written that down.

Six minutes.

He thought about his mother on a gravel bar four years ago with his brother on her back, looking at the ugliest blade anybody in the valley had ever pulled out of a river stone. *Well, it looks like you. That's the whole of it. Don't spend too long.*

He had never asked her what she meant by the last part. He had thought he had years.

Seven.

The dark in the bucket came down to the line.

---

He could feel exactly where it was. That was the strange thing. He had never known where anything was in that bucket before, and now he knew the way you know the edge of a step in the dark with your foot: there. A finger from the bottom. If it went below that, he would not be able to get up off this slab afterward. He would not be able to light the knife tomorrow. He would be the one somebody had to carry.

He took his hand off her chest. Only a little. Only to see.

She went grey.

It happened under his hand, in the time it took to breathe out. The heart that had settled to a beat went back toward the bird, and the breath went high, and the shaking stopped, and the Elder's voice in his head said *a child that's gone quiet and easy in the cold is a child two hours from the end of it*, and he did not think she had two hours. He did not think she had one.

He put his hand back.

It was not a decision. He had thought, when Dee drew the line on the inside of his bucket, that if he ever came to it there would be a decision, a place where he stood and weighed one thing against the other and chose. There was no place like that. There was a woman on a slab who went grey when he took his hand away, and did not when he put it back, and there was a line a finger from the bottom of a bucket.

*You don't get out of owing*, he had told his brother, in the ward, with the squirrel four hundred miles north. *You just have to find a different heap.*

He had not thought it would be a ditch.

He went below the line.

---

It was cold.

That was the first thing. The cold came in through his hands and feet first, the way it had in the snow. His fingers on her chest went cold from the ends inward, and his feet in the black water, which had been cold already, went a different kind of cold, the kind that is not in the skin but under it. Then it was in his wrists. Then his shins.

It was not like the bucket going down. Above the line it had been water going out of a bucket. Below it, it was the bucket.

He did not stop.

Eight minutes. Nine.

The fog was thinning. He noticed it the way you notice things at the edge of a fever. It had gone from white to grey to a sort of wet silver, and he could see further than his hand now, he could see the white post with the six on it leaning out of the water beside the slab, and he could see its shadow.

It had two.

And past it, at the edge of the fog, east, where the ditch came in, something was standing very still, side-on, with its head low.

---

It was long in the body and short in the leg and the colour of wet ash, and the light came off its back with a shiver in it, the way air shivers over a hot stone.

There was another behind it.

They were not moving. They were doing the other thing, the stillness, the thing the first one had done in the ditch on the north road at thirty feet, from lope to stillness with nothing in between. They were not looking at him so much as standing there being aimed at him.

He understood it all at once, the way he had understood a fence.

They had gone into the pipe because the pipe was dark. Something had stopped them in the pipe; he did not know what. Callie, he thought, at the west mouth, with her hook. They had come back out of it, east, into the ditch, into the fog, looking for dark. And the whole of the Sag was lit now: Toren's spike in the door, Katori's grey blade somewhere, lamps on poles on the east track, Ines on the rim. Everywhere was lit.

Except here.

Here there was a boy kneeling in black water with his knife gone back into the metal on his neck cord, and a woman on her back on a slab with his hand on her chest, and no light at all. The darkest thing in the Sag.

*They come at the dark bit.*

He could not light the knife. He knew that without trying. There was nothing to light it with. Everything that had been in the bucket and everything that had been the bucket was going down his arm into her chest, and if he took any of it back for the knife she would go grey, and if he took his hand off her to hold the knife up she would go grey, and there were two of them and one of him and she could not move.

*They can only come at one place*, he had thought on the north road. *So pick it.*

He was the place.

"Mine," he said.

He did not say it loud. He did not have anything to say it loud with. He said it to the two shapes at the edge of the fog, the way he had shouted it on the north road into the dark with his brother behind him, only now it was not a shout, it was barely a word. *Mine. They come at me.*

The near one dropped its head.

---

There was a crack like struts loading, and a scatter of grit off the edge of the slab, and somebody came out of the fog from the west faster than a person could run.

He came badly. His right boot skidded on the wet stone when he stopped and he nearly went over, and then did not. He stopped from his heels. He stopped with his weight going down into the slab, the way a boy on a sand heap had made Otto stand a hundred times, a pace in front of Jab and the woman, between them and the edge of the fog.

He had one arm in a sling against his chest. On the other fist there was a crooked, ugly spike, thick in the heel, and along the inner curve of it lay a thin line of light, hard and white-grey and cold.

It did not shake.

Toren stood on the dropped slab with his light held out low across the space between the hounds and Jab, the way you hold a lantern across a gate, and did not move.

*Light where you don't want them.*

The near hound came off it like water off a plate. It slid. It went round to the right, away from the white-grey line, and the second one went with it, the way a flock of birds turns without anybody deciding, and Toren moved his light a hand's width to the right, not more, and they came back left, exactly as far, and stopped.

They did not come at him. They had never once come at the light.

And behind them, out of the grey at the edge of the fog, something small and fast came over the lip of the east ditch with a grey blade lying flat and lit along it, like a stove across a room, and the near hound was going one way and the ash of it was going another, and the small shape was already turning.

Two seconds. He counted it afterward. One of them was her getting up over the lip of the ditch.

The second hound came apart where it stood.

Katori stood in the east ditch up to her knees in black water with her grey blade lit, and looked at Jab, and at the woman under his hand, and at Toren in front of them with his light still held out across nothing.

"Where's the medic," she said. It was not a question. It was Katori saying a thing out loud so that somebody would hear it.

"Coming," said Toren. He did not take his eyes off the fog. "Dessa sent her. Wyck asked where the holder was. Nobody had her on the count."

---

She came out of the fog from the south at a run.

She came down the road from the lip, in her oilskin, with the canvas bag with the red cross on the flap banging against her hip and two men from the long building behind her with a stretcher between them, and she came down off the edge of the made road onto the dropped slab without slowing, into the water, and knelt.

She looked at the day holder for about four seconds without touching her. Then she looked at Jab's hand on the woman's chest. Then she looked at Jab's face.

"How long?" she said.

"Nine minutes," said Jab. "Ten. I counted her breaths." His voice was not doing what he told it. "Something's broken inside. Left side. Under the ribs. It's bleeding somewhere it shouldn't. I'm not touching that. I'm only—"

"I know what you're only," said the woman with the good boots. "Off."

He did not move his hand.

"Jab. Off."

"She goes grey," he said. "If I take it off. I tried. She goes grey."

The woman put her own hand flat on the day holder's chest, beside his. Her palm was cold from the run. He felt it arrive.

And then he felt what came out of it.

It was the same. It was warmth, the plainest thing in the world, going down through a palm into a chest. But it was not coming from her. He could feel that. He had felt it once before, in the shed, with his palm on a dish: the difference between a room with somebody asleep in it and an empty room. What was coming out of her hand into the day holder's chest was coming from an empty room. There was nobody in it. It was clean and cold and it belonged to nobody, and it went into the woman under their hands and did exactly what his had done, and the woman with the good boots knelt in the black water with it going out of her and did not go grey, or cold, or anything at all.

She was full. She stayed full.

"I've got her," she said. "Let go."

He could not.

She took his wrist in her other hand, the one with the lump on the outside where a bone had knit wrong, and lifted his hand off the day holder's chest, the way you lift a sleeping child's hand off a blanket.

The day holder did not go grey.

Jab fell sideways onto the wet slab.

---

He did not lose the whole of it. He lost most.

He was lying on his side on tilted stone with black water round his face, and he could see, a long way off, the woman with the good boots kneeling with her hand flat on the day holder's chest, steady, and the two men from the long building unfolding the stretcher, and Toren's boots, and Katori's. He could see the woman's face. It was calm. She was breathing the way you breathe when you are doing a thing you have done a great many times, in and out, even. She was not emptying. He could see she was not emptying.

*That's what I'd have needed*, he thought, very slowly, as if the thought were being carried to him from a long way off by somebody walking. *That. Whatever that is.*

Somebody was saying his name.

He was very cold. The cold was in his hands and feet and wrists and shins and now it was in his chest, under his breastbone, a finger's width behind it, in the place where the bucket was. He went down to look at the bucket. He always went down to look.

There was no bucket.

There was no bottom to find. There was no line scratched on the inside a finger up from the bottom. There was nothing there at all, not dry, not empty, not anything, the way there is nothing where a step should be in the dark.

"Jab," said somebody. Toren. "*Jab.*"

He tried to answer. He found he was not there to do it.

---

The first thing he knew was a bar.

It hung over him on a chain, a short smooth bar like the rail of a gate, and it was swinging a little, and on the end of it there was a hand. A small hand, holding on.

He turned his head.

He was in a bed. It was a white bed in a long room with a window over it, and there was a light in the ceiling that did not flicker, and the room smelled of soap and of something sharper underneath. He knew the room. He had sat in a chair in it for twenty-five days.

He had never been in one of the beds.

In the next bed, the fifth on the right, under its frame, his brother was sitting up.

Tonk had both hands on the bar that hung over his own bed, and he had pulled himself up on it until he was sitting, with his back off the pillow and his shoulders up and his face bright red with effort and outrage, and he was looking over at Jab with an expression Jab had never seen on him.

"You were *asleep*," said Tonk.

Jab looked at him.

"You were asleep for *ages*," said Tonk. "They brought you in on a *bed*. With *wheels*. You were all wet and your mouth was open and you didn't say anything, and I said *Jab*, and you didn't *say* anything." His voice went up. "So I sat up. By *myself*. On the bar. The doctor said a little further every day and I did it all at *once*, because you were *asleep*."

"You sat up," said Jab.

"By *myself*."

"On your own."

"*Yes*," said Tonk, enormously.

Jab lay in the white bed and looked at his brother sitting up, and something went through him that was two things at once, and he did not know which was bigger. The first was so big it filled his chest like water filling a boot. His brother was sitting up. His brother, who had lain flat on his back on boards and blankets and a carrying frame and a padded trough for ten weeks, was sitting up on his own under a bar, looking down at him.

The second was smaller and sharper and went in under the first, like a splinter under a nail. *He did it without me.* The first time. The thing Keel said he would do a little at a time, every day, the thing Jab had thought he would be sitting in the chair for, watching; and it had happened while Jab was lying on a wheeled bed with his mouth open, and he had missed it.

"That's further," he said. His voice did not work well. "That's much further."

"I *know*," said Tonk.

---

There was something round his wrist.

He looked down. It was the long cord. The twelve-foot one, the one from the road, washed and tagged at the hatch and knotted every night since the second day to Tonk's wrist at one end and the bed rail or Jab's wrist at the other. Tonk's end was on Tonk's wrist, where it always was. The other end was not on Tonk's rail.

It was tied to Jab's.

Somebody had tied it with a knot he did not know, clumsy and huge, a knot tied by somebody who had never tied a knot before and had been determined to make it hold.

"The nurse did the knot," said Tonk. "I told her how. She did it wrong. So I told her again." He looked at the cord with great satisfaction. "So you don't go *anywhere*."

On the pillow beside Jab's head, where he had not noticed it, there was a small black wooden fish with one fin broken off.

"Little's minding you," said Tonk.

Jab put his hand up, slowly, because his arm was very heavy and very cold, and touched the fish with one finger.

In the fourth bed, on the other side of Tonk, Senna lay on her back with her hands folded and her eyes on the ceiling.

"He's been sitting up for an hour," she said, to the ceiling. "He said he'd not lie down till you woke. I told him that was a great deal of sitting for a boy with nails in his leg. He told me I wasn't his gran."

"She's *not*," said Tonk.

"I'm not," Senna agreed. "I've got one of my own. He's no better behaved."

---

Rook was in a chair at the foot of the bed.

Jab had not seen him. He was sitting very straight with his back not touching the chair and his forearms across his knees and his hands hanging off the ends of them, and his hands were shaking. They were shaking the way they shook on the north lip every morning after his hour, a fine fast tremor from the wrists to the fingertips, only worse, and he was not hiding them, and his face was the colour of the white bed.

"Is she—" said Jab.

"Alive," said Rook.

Jab shut his eyes.

"Sowerby had her open by the ninth hour," said Rook. "There was a thing in her side that had torn, that nobody needs. He took it out. He says she'll live." His voice was very tired. "He says she'd have been dead before the stretcher got to the lip. Without the nine minutes. He wanted me to tell you that. He says he'll tell you himself, and you won't hear him, because nobody hears the good thing the first time."

"She won't hold," said Jab. It was not a question.

"Not this winter. Maybe not again. Sowerby doesn't know. She doesn't either." Rook looked at his hands. "She's asked for her coat. They won't give it to her."

"The girl. With the wrist."

"Broken. Clean. Keel set it before noon. Keel says she fell right, and the girl's told everybody in the long building that she fell right, and that Wyck Tanner taught her, and Wyck Tanner's been sitting on the step of the shed all afternoon pretending he didn't hear about it." Something moved at the corner of Rook's mouth and did not get any further. "Toren's opened his hand again, the seam across the palm, holding that spike. Keel's stitched it. His arm's no worse, which she says is a miracle, and she doesn't hold with them."

"Callie?"

"At her floor," said Rook. "She stopped."

Jab lay still.

"She stopped," he said.

"The hounds left the pipe when Toren put his light across the mouth of it. She held the hook to her floor, counting, and when she got there she said so, out loud, and Katori came down the rim and said *out*, and she took it out." Rook's hands shook on the ends of his arms. "She's in fourteen with her back against the stove, and a finger of something left, and her mother's hand in hers."

"I didn't stop," said Jab.

"No," said Rook. "You didn't."

---

They did not say anything for a while.

Down the far end of the ward the nurse turned the small lamp lower. It was dark outside the window. Jab did not know what hour it was. He did not know what day. He found he did not want to ask.

"You held it," he said. "All of it. The bottom. When she went."

"I did."

"How long?"

"Thirty-one minutes," said Rook. "Until the fog was off it and the grey ones were down and the crew was up and there was nothing left in the bottom but her blood on a slab. Then I let it go." He looked at his hands. "Sowerby tried to put me in a bed. I said no. He said a word I hadn't heard a doctor say. I've been in this chair since."

"You shouldn't have."

"No," said Rook. "Nor should you."

Jab looked at the bar hanging over his brother's bed, swinging a little on its chain. Tonk had let go of it at last and lain back down, and was lying with his eyes open and the cord on his wrist, listening, the way he listened to everything, to file it.

"You said to ask you again," said Jab. "About what it is. In a while."

"I did."

"You said when I'd seen both halves do something nobody set up."

"I did."

"Is it a while?"

Rook looked at him. He looked at him for a long time from the chair at the foot of the bed, with his shaking hands hanging off the ends of his arms and his face the colour of the sheet.

"It's a while," he said.

---

"It's Sustain," said Rook. "All of it."

Jab did not say anything.

"The lean," said Rook. "That wakes you at the second hour and tells you your brother's worse. The warmth, that went into him at the culvert on the north road and into him in the snow. What you did today on that slab, in the fog, for a woman you'd never spoken to, at forty yards, before you could see her." He did not raise his voice. "You thought they were two things. You told her, in the shed. The lean and the warmth. You said it opened like an ear, not like a hand."

"I said that on the road. To myself."

"She wrote it down. She showed me." Rook's mouth moved. "She writes everything down. It's the best thing about her and it's the worst."

"Is it two things?"

"No," said Rook. "It's one. The ear's the hand. It's the same hand, reaching. The lean is it reaching before you know it's reaching; it goes out ahead of you, and it finds what's worse, and it tells you. The warmth is it arriving. That's all. It goes, and it gets there." He looked at Jab steadily. "Today it went forty yards through fog for somebody you didn't know, and got there, and held her up for nine minutes while her blood went where it shouldn't. That isn't two doors. That's one door, going and arriving. It's always been one. You just never saw it go that far before, because you only ever sent it to one place."

Jab lay with his hand on the fish on his pillow.

"How do you know?" he said.

"Because I've done it," said Rook. "Not like you. Worse than you. With less." He looked at his hands. "On a riverbank, with a girl of fourteen with a broken wrist and a fever that wouldn't come down, four days' walk from anywhere. I put my hand on her four nights running and went down past my floor every night and didn't know I had one. The fifth night I couldn't get up. She kept me breathing with her hands till morning. She was fourteen, and she didn't know how, and she did it anyway." He was quiet for a while. "I never had a word for it either. I had the doing. I've had the doing for half my life. She came back off the river with the word."

Jab thought about a woman with a crooked wrist kneeling in black water, calm, breathing evenly, with warmth going out of her hand that belonged to nobody.

"She didn't empty," he said. "Today. When she put her hand on the holder. I watched. She didn't go cold. She stayed full."

"No," said Rook. "She had something else to spend."

---

"I'd give her the same again," said Jab. "If it was tomorrow."

"I know you would."

"I'm not sorry."

"I didn't ask you to be." Rook leaned forward a little in his chair, and the shaking in his hands got worse, and he let it. "Listen. I'm going to say this once, because she'll say it better tomorrow and you'll want to have heard it from me first. It's not that you should have given her less. You gave her what she needed, and she's alive, and nothing anybody says in a shed takes that off you. It's that you gave her the only thing you had. You gave her you. Because there wasn't anything else in your hands to give." He sat back. "Next time there'll be something else in your hands. That's all she's trying to teach you. Not to care less. To carry more."

Jab lay still for a long time.

Then he went down, under his breastbone, a finger's width behind it, to look at the bucket.

There was nothing there. There was no bucket. There was only a place where a bucket had been, and cold.

"I can't find it," he said. His voice did something. "It's not empty. It's not there. I go down to look and there's nothing to look at."

"I know."

"When does it come back?"

"When it's ready," said Rook.

"What if it doesn't?"

Rook looked at him from the foot of the bed.

"Don't check," he said.

Jab turned his head on the pillow. In the next bed, his brother was lying with his eyes open in the dark under the frame, with the long cord running from his wrist across the gap between the beds to Jab's rail.

"Lie *down*," said Tonk. "I'm doing the sitting. You do the lying."

"I am lying down."

"Lie down *more*," said Tonk, and pulled the cord, once, a small pull.

Jab was already there.

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-16.md

# Chapter 16 — Callie: Something Left

She woke with a finger of water in the well, and knew it before she opened her eyes.

It was the first thing she did every morning now. Before the stove, before her boots, before she looked at her mother. She went down into herself the way her grandfather had gone down a ladder into a grain bin, carefully, one rung at a time, and looked. This morning there was a finger of cold clear water lying in the bottom, above the place where the floor was, and it had not been there when she lay down. It had come back in the night while she was asleep, slowly, on its own, the way it always did.

She had kept it. She had stood in a ditch with her feet in black water and a pale hook across the mouth of a pipe, with nothing on her at all, and counted to her floor, and stopped.

She lay on the floor by the stove and let herself know that for a while.

Her ribs hurt. They hurt the way they had hurt on the hill, deep, all down the left side, where the pipe had tried to go out through her hands when the Ward left the hook at the jam and again yesterday when the first hound hit it from inside. Her nose had bled twice in the night. There was a crust of it on her lip.

"Something left?" said her mother, from the bed.

Callie had not known she was awake.

"A finger," said Callie.

Her mother held out her left hand, palm up, over the edge of the bed, and Callie reached up from the floor and put her own hand in it. They stayed like that for a while, with the stove ticking, and the blanket over the window, and Katori asleep on her face in the next bed with her coat on.

"Good," said her mother. "Now tell me the rest. I've had it from four people and they all tell it wrong."

---

So Callie told it. She told it lying on the floor with her hand in her mother's, once, in order, the way she had told the Director's office.

She told the warm iron at post five, and the two shadows, and Toren saying *I've seen that before*, and the slab going. She told the fog sitting up. She told Katori ten paces above them with her grey blade going, and the husks coming up between, and Katori seeing she could not get down, and saying *West*, and going up with the rest of the crew because that was her job. She told the bottom of the Sag like a lake of milk, and the weighbridge girl going down and falling right, and Toren hauling her up by the arm with the heel of his ugly spike. She told Jab on the open slope with his whole knife lit, calling the husks off them like water to a drain.

She told the water stopping.

"I knew they were in the pipe," she said. "Because the water wasn't coming out. It had been coming out for six days, since the rain. You could hear it from the rim. And it stopped. Something big enough was in there to stop it." She looked at the stove. "And they hadn't come at the light. The husks came at the light. Whatever came up after them didn't. So it had gone to the dark. And the only dark between the east ditch and the door was the pipe."

"So you put your hook in it."

"Across it. Like the rail at the jam. With the Ward in it." Callie was quiet a moment. "And then I had nothing on me."

Her mother's fingers tightened.

"It hit the hook," said Callie. "From inside. It sounded like somebody throwing a sack of stones at a gate. The hook didn't move. Nothing moves the hook when it's in there. So everything moved round it." She looked at the ceiling. "Toren came down and stood beside me with his light, low, across the mouth. They went back from the light. And then Jab called him up to the door, and he went, and it was me and the hook and Ines on the rim with one arrow."

"And you counted."

"I counted." Callie shut her eyes. "At about thirty they went quiet in the pipe. Dessa says the thing that goes quiet is the thing that isn't coming. I didn't believe her. I kept the hook in. At fifty I could feel the floor coming up. At fifty-eight I was on it."

"And every part of you wanted to go on."

Callie opened her eyes.

"Yes," she said.

"Because what if they came back."

"Yes."

"And you didn't."

"Katori came down the rim," said Callie. "She said *out*. I'd have said it myself. I think. I'd got as far as opening my mouth." She let her breath go. "I took it out. The hook went dark. They'd gone. They'd gone east, back through the pipe, into the fog, to the dark. To where Jab was. I didn't know that. Nobody knew that."

Her mother did not say anything for a long time.

"I'm very proud of you," she said at last, "and I'm very angry about all of it, and I'd like you to hold my hand a bit longer while I decide which one I'm going to be for the rest of the morning."

Callie held it a bit longer.

---

The lift was lost.

She found that out at the weighbridge, at the middle of the morning, when she walked her mother over for the tally. The drift below post five, the one the night crew had called a Flask and three Handfuls, had gone down with the slab into black water at the bottom of the dip and was there still, a smear with the light crawling through it under four feet of ditch. The canvas bag Toren had dropped on the east track had been picked up by the night crew in the afternoon: eleven jars, five broken, the ash from the broken ones spread through the frost and the grit and the glass, dirty past lifting.

The board on the wall of the weighbridge hut had the twenty-fifth day painted along the top in white, and under it, against every name, nothing at all.

"Nobody gets paid for a lift that went in the ditch," said the old man in the apron. He said it without any feeling, the way he said a weight. "That's the rule. It's always been the rule."

"It's a bad rule," said Callie's mother, climbing onto her stool.

"It's a rule," said the old man. He put the test weights on the plate, one and then the other, and went back into the hut and looked at the beam. Then he came out again and stood beside the stool. "I'd have paid it," he said. "If it were mine. It isn't."

Callie looked at the board. Her own name was there, painted in white, with nothing after it. There would be nothing after it on the sheet at the review. A lost day, and the day before that when she could not kneel. Two days out of the last five.

She thought of her mother's rule, and of a floor kept with the blood running out of her nose, and did not know whether she had spent the day or saved it. She thought that was probably the question the woman with the good boots had been trying to get at all along.

---

They brought Jab across to the shed in Senna's chair in the afternoon.

Toren pushed. He pushed one-handed, with his right palm freshly stitched where the seam had opened again round the spike, and a clean white wrapping over it, and he leaned his weight on the handle to steer, and Senna had lent the chair on the condition, relayed by Toren word for word, that it was Tuesday for somebody even if it was not Tuesday for her.

Jab sat in it wrapped in two blankets with his hands in his lap. Callie had seen people come off the road looking like that. She had looked like that, on the tailboard, the first night on the causeway, with the Ward on her for thirteen hours and nothing to eat. Grey, and very still, and cold all the way through in a way that did not come from outside. His hands shook in his lap. He did not hide them either. He had learned that off Rook, Callie thought, or off the day holder, or off everybody in this yard at once.

He looked at her when the chair came in through the door.

"You stopped," he said.

"I stopped."

He looked at her a long time. There was nothing in his face she could read; she had tried, the way she read ground, and found only the fog. Then he nodded, once.

"Good," he said.

---

The woman with the good boots had the brass scale out on the table, and the horn spoon, and one of the three jars from the rack by the gate, grey to the shoulder.

Rook was sitting on the cot. His hands were shaking so hard that he had put them flat on his knees to keep them still, and they would not keep still. He had held the whole bottom of the Sag alone for thirty-one minutes, and then sat in a chair at the foot of a bed all night, and Sowerby had come to the shed at noon and said a number of things to him in front of everybody in a voice Callie had never heard Sowerby use, and Rook had sat there and let him.

"Everybody who can see, look," said the woman. "This is the thing I should have shown you on the first morning in this yard. I didn't, because I thought there was time." She did not look at Jab. "That's mine. I'll write it down."

She weighed out a quarter of a Handful. She did it slowly, with the spoon, tipping grey powder from the jar into the little pan until the beam came level, and then she tipped the pan out onto a square of paper, and it was a heap Callie could not have hidden under her thumb. A heap the size of the top of a thumb.

"Three," she said. "Take in a little that you've measured."

She put her palm flat on it. Callie watched the grey go up into her hand, not fast, not slow. At the rate it came. The paper was clean when she lifted her palm.

"Four," said the woman. "Let it settle. Don't touch it." She sat down on the edge of the table with her hands in her lap. "Callie. Count for me. My breaths. I'll need sixty."

Callie counted.

It was the strangest thing she had ever counted. She counted the woman's breathing, in and out, slow and even, the way she had counted her mother's on the plain every night for a month and had stopped counting, on the floor by the stove, because it did not need her. This did. The woman had asked for it. It was the kind of counting that was for somebody else.

At forty the woman shut her eyes. At fifty she opened them.

"Sixty," said Callie.

"It's settled," said the woman. "It's in me. It isn't me. I know which is which, because I've done this ten thousand times since I was eleven, in a hut with a very small old man called Orrin Vell standing over me with a stick, and I can tell you exactly where it's lying and exactly where I start." She looked at Jab. "You can tell too. You told me on the first morning. You put your hand on a dish and said *mine's got somebody in it*. Remember that."

Jab said nothing.

The woman got up and went to the cot and knelt in front of Rook, and put both her hands flat over the tops of his, where they lay shaking on his knees.

"Don't," said Rook.

"Too late," said the woman.

---

Callie watched Rook's hands stop.

They did not stop all at once. They slowed, the way the woman had slowed them on the north lip on the twelfth day, like a bell slowing after it is struck, the long fine tremor going longer and finer until she had to look for it, and then it was not there. Rook's shoulders came down half an inch. He shut his eyes.

The woman knelt there with her hands on his for as long as it took Callie to count thirty. Then she took them away and stood up, and went back to the table, and picked up the little brass glass with the sand in it.

"I measured my floor at first light," she said. "Before any of this. Two turns and a half, Ward, standing. I'm going to measure it again."

She stood in the middle of the shed with her feet planted and a shine came over her like old glass, a skin's width off her grey coat, and Dessa turned the glass. The sand ran. Callie watched it go, a thin grey thread through the waist of the glass, and pile up, and the woman stood in her Ward with her face calm and her eyes on the stove, and Dessa turned the glass again, and the sand ran through again, and the woman stood.

At two turns and a half, the shine went off her.

"Floor," she said. "Same as this morning. I've spent a quarter of a Handful through Sustain into a man who can't get the angle on himself, and I'm standing exactly where I was standing at first light." She put the glass down. "That's what the jar's for."

---

Nobody said anything for a while. The bucket at the far end of the shed went *tick*.

"Yesterday you had nothing to spend but you," said the woman to Jab.

She said it gently, for her. Callie had not heard her say anything gently before.

"You went across the bottom of the Sag in the fog because something in you told you a woman was dying there, and it was right, and nobody else knew. And you got to her, and she was dying, and you held her up. For nine minutes. On nothing. On you." She pulled a chair round with her foot and sat on it, in front of him, so that their faces were level. "I had a Handful already in me when I came down that road. Settled. I'd taken it in at the gate before first light, because that's what you do on a lift day, because Vell made us do it before every lift from the first week, and I've never once not done it since. So when I got to you I had something to spend that wasn't me. You didn't. And you didn't because I hadn't taught you how." She did not look away from him. "That's not yours. That's mine."

Jab looked at his hands.

"You'd have spent the same," she said. "I know. You'd give her the same again tomorrow. I'm not asking you not to. I'm asking you to have a jar when you do it. And somebody standing behind you whose job it is, written down, to take your wrist and lift your hand off when you get to your floor. And somebody next to you who can take over when they do." She sat back. "That's all it is. Skill, so you know which is which. Something in your hands besides you. Somebody to take your place. And a line, and somebody who'll pull you back from it. Four things."

"You pulled my hand off," said Jab.

"Somebody has to," said the woman. "Next time we'll write down who, before we go."

"Not so I'll care less," said Jab slowly. Callie could see him turning it over, the way she had seen him turn over a jam from the bottom up.

"No," said the woman. "So you're still standing for the next one."

---

Rook opened his eyes.

"She's right," he said.

He said it to the room, not to her. He sat on the cot with his hands flat on his knees, still for the first time since the Sag, and looked at the stove.

"I found my floor on a riverbank four nights running and didn't know it was there. I found it on the Level, and on the north road, and yesterday for thirty-one minutes with a road shut under me and nobody to take the other end. I've never once known where it was till I was under it." He was quiet. "She's right. I'd have taught him the doing. I'd never have taught him to come back from it. I don't know how."

"He found her," said the woman with the good boots.

Rook looked at her.

"I was at the gate," she said. "Where the Director put me. Two hundred paces. When the three blasts went, I came to the lip, with the kit and the stores and the men and the stretcher. And there was a girl there with her wrist broken, and I did her wrist, because she was there and she was hurt and nobody on the lip had anybody else on their count." She did not look at anybody. "Dessa had the lifters. Dessa had Callie and Toren and the girl. Nobody had the holder. The holder was the line. Nobody counts the line." She was quiet a moment. "I'd have been two minutes late. If Wyck hadn't said *where's the holder*. I'd have been two minutes late with a Handful settled in me and the jars at my feet, and she'd have been dead on that slab. He found her. He knew. With nothing."

"You'd have been in time for the next one," said Rook.

"That's the point of me," said the woman.

They looked at each other across the shed, a man of thirty on a cot with his hands still and a woman of twenty-five on a chair with a crooked wrist, and Callie thought that the argument that had started on some road she had never seen was not finished. It had not been won. It had changed shape, the way a jam changes shape when you take the key out of it and everything that was held comes loose at once and goes somewhere new.

"Write it down," said Senna's voice, from nowhere.

Everybody turned. Senna was not there. She was in the fourth bed on the right in the long building, four hundred yards away, flat on her back with her hands folded. It was Dessa who had said it, in Senna's voice, without meaning to, sitting at her table with the pencil in her hand.

Dessa went red.

"She'd have said it," said Dessa. "If she was here. So I've said it."

"Then write it," said the woman with the good boots.

Dessa wrote it.

---

"Could it hold the hook longer?" said Callie.

They looked at her.

"The jar," said Callie. "What you did. Settled. Could it go into the hook? Instead of me? So I could keep a Ward in the steel past my floor, without going under it?"

The woman looked at her for a long moment.

"Any door," she said. "It's the same fuel. It doesn't care whose door it goes through. Edge, Ward, Sustain, his struts, Wyck's standing still." She turned the brass glass over in her fingers, and did not set it running. "Not yet. You can't spend what you haven't learned to settle, and you've settled once, a tenth, sitting down in a shed. You'd be the one holding the hook with a Handful sloshing about in you on a road with things coming at you." She put the glass down. "Not yet."

"But yes."

"Not yet," said the woman, "is a kind of yes. It's the kind that keeps people alive long enough to hear the rest of it."

Callie filed it. She put it away in the same place she kept the grade on a sack and the gap on the handoff and the eleven seconds with Wyck, the way you put a tool in a bag you mean to carry.

---

The next morning somebody had chalked a word on the board at the weighbridge.

Callie saw it when she walked her mother over for the tally. The painted names ran down one side of the board in white, and the chalked weights down the other, and the twenty-fifth day was still blank all the way down. But against the line for hut twelve, where Jab's name was painted and had a Flask of hound ash chalked after it from the north road night, somebody had written a word in the space for the weight, in big, careful, uneven capitals, the way a person writes who does not write very often.

*SUSSTAIN.*

"That wasn't me," said her mother, climbing onto her stool.

"I know."

"It wasn't him either," said her mother, nodding at the old man in the apron. "He'd have spelt it right. There's only one *s* in the middle and he knows it."

The old man looked at the board for a long time.

"Night crew," he said at last. "They came through at the fifth hour for the test weights. They were talking about it all the way across the yard." He did not rub it out. "The whole Sag knows. The lad from twelve went into the fog in the dark and found her. Nobody else did."

A woman came to the window of the hut at the middle of the morning. She was not a lifter. She was a Meridian woman in a knitted shawl, about her mother's age, with her hands twisted together, and she did not look at the plate or the beam or the jars. She looked at Callie, because Callie was standing nearest.

"Which hut's the boy?" she said. "The one who kept the holder. They said at the pump he's in the second row." She twisted her hands. "My girl's had a fever three days. It won't break. They said he'd know."

Callie looked at her.

"He's in the long building," she said. "In a bed. He can't do anything. He can't find it." She did not know how else to say it. "Go to Mrs Lask at the desk. She'll send you to Doctor Sowerby. He's better than Jab at fevers. Jab would tell you so himself."

"They said he'd know," said the woman again. She said it as if it were the only thing she had come with, and she could not put it down.

"He'd know she was worse," said Callie. "He wouldn't be able to do anything about it. Not today." She thought about the bucket Jab had told her about on the steps, that he went down to look at every night, and could not find. "Go to the desk."

The woman went. Callie watched her go across the yard toward the long building with her shawl pulled round her, walking fast, and thought: *there'll be more of them.* She did not know how she knew. She knew it the way she knew a husk line: not a track so much as a direction. Once a thing like that got onto a board in chalk, it did not come off.

---

The Director came to the shed at noon.

She came across the yard on foot with the man in the long coat a pace behind her, small and square in a grey coat with her spectacles on their cord, and people in the yard stopped what they were doing and watched her come, because nobody had ever seen her come down the outside stair in the middle of a working day. She came in at the shed door without knocking and stood inside it, looking at the stove, and the cot, and the bucket under the drip, and the table with the brass scale, and Jab, in Senna's chair again, with his two blankets.

"You're the one," she said to him.

Jab looked at her.

"Sowerby says she'd have been dead before the stretcher got to the lip," said the Director. "Without you. He put it in writing, which he never does, and sent it up the stair, which he never does either." She took her spectacles off. "So I'll say it to you once, in person, because it should be said to your face and not on a card. Thank you. The station owes you a woman who's held a road for it for two years with nobody watching. It knows it does."

"I'd like her coat given back," said Jab.

The Director looked at him.

"She's asked for it," said Jab. "Rook says they won't give it to her."

The Director looked at him a moment longer. Then she turned her head a quarter inch toward the man in the long coat, and the man in the long coat wrote something.

"Done," said the Director.

---

Then she took a buff card out of her coat.

It was not like the cards that had come under the doors. It was bigger, and there was more written on it, in a neat hand that was not the Director's, and there was a line at the bottom for a name.

"I'll tell you what I want," said the Director. "And then what I'll take."

She did not put the card down. She held it.

"I want you on the medical register. Not the long line with one name on it. The roll of the long building, with Sowerby and Keel and Miss Wren. I want your household settled for four years, not twenty-eight days. A hut by the long building, not in Yard C, four hundred yards from your brother. Training under Miss Wren, every day, on whatever terms she sets." She paused. "And in return, I want you on call to the long building. When a thing comes through the gate that's worse, and there's nobody else. That's what I want."

The shed was very quiet.

Callie watched her. She watched her hold the card without putting it down, and she watched her face, which did nothing, and she thought of her grandfather at the horse fair at Thistle Ford again, walking round a horse he had already decided to buy. She had thought, on the second floor, that the Director looked at Jab like a buyer. She had been wrong. That had been a buyer looking. This was a buyer who had seen the horse run.

"Not while I'm like this," said Jab.

"No," said the Director. "I know. I've got eyes." She put the card back in her coat. "I'll ask again."

"And if I say no when you do?"

"Then you'll have said no," said the Director. "And it'll be on your sheet that you did, in the margin, the way you like it." She put her spectacles on. "I'll not pretend it goes away, Mr—" She stopped. "Jab. I'll not pretend. Yesterday this station found out what it had in hut twelve. The whole Sag knows. There's a word in chalk on my weighbridge board that I didn't put there and can't have rubbed off without every crew in the north yard asking why. I'd be lying if I told you the station was going to forget. It never forgets anything. That's the trouble with it."

"That's a column now," said Pell, from the doorway, very quietly, to nobody.

The Director turned her head and looked at him.

"Yes," she said. "It is."

---

Dessa showed her the book that evening, when the yard was empty.

She did not show anybody else. She waited until the others had gone, and Callie was the last, putting the hook over her shoulder at the door, and Dessa said "Callie," in her flat voice, and turned the book round on the table so that it faced her.

It was the page for the twenty-fifth day.

It went across both sides of the book, the way the map had, and Dessa had ruled it into columns in pencil. *What we did.* *What went wrong.* *Who.* *How long.* *What it cost.* *Recovery.* *Second attempt.* *What to change.* She had filled in every column except one.

*Supervised lift. Minute nought to minute twenty-one: lift as planned.* Under it: *Warm iron, post 5. Two shadows — T. Slab at 6 going — C. Lifters moved back ten paces — K. Minute twenty-one: slab dropped. Line folded, east corner. Husks up. Lifters below post 5 cut off: C., T., the girl. Across the bottom to the door. Girl down, wrist; fell right — Wyck's lane. Hounds went to the dark, into the pipe. C.: hook across the west mouth, Ward in it, fifty-eight seconds, to her floor, stopped. T.: light at the pipe; then the door; light held through a fall, first time. J.: lit to call the husks off; then left the door to T.; went across the bottom dark.*

And under that, in capitals, pressed so hard the pencil had nearly gone through:

*THE HOLDER WAS HIT AT THE FOLD. IN THE FOG. NOBODY SAW.*

*J. felt her. Forty yards. Before he could see her. Held her nine minutes. Below his floor. Floor: a finger.*

*R.: the whole bottom, alone, thirty-one minutes.*

*D. W.: at the gate. Came at three blasts. Did the girl's wrist first; nobody had the holder on the count. Came down at Wyck's word. Took over from J. on a settled Handful. Stayed at her floor.*

In the column for what it cost, Dessa had written: *A Flask and three Handfuls, in the ditch. Eleven jars, five broken. The girl's wrist. T.'s palm. R.: flat. J.: can't find it. C.: a finger. The holder: her spleen, and the winter.*

In the column for *second attempt*, there was nothing.

In the column for *what to change*, there were a great many things, in several hands. Some of them were Pell's, small and neat. Some were the woman with the good boots', square. One was in letters big and bad, from shaking hands. Callie read them all.

*Somebody at the bottom who can feel where people are. Not at the lip. Thirty feet isn't the bottom of a dip.* That was Dessa's own hand. *The medic at the lip. Argue it again.* *Both ends of the pipe.* *Floors checked at the twentieth minute, not only at the gate.* *A jar for every healer. Settled before they go down.* *Somebody whose job it is to lift the hand off. Written down, by name, before.*

And the last one, in the big bad letters: *COUNT THE HOLDER.*

---

Callie read it twice. Then she looked up.

"There's nothing in *second attempt*," she said.

"No," said Dessa. "There hasn't been one."

"There'll be one."

Dessa looked at the empty column for a long time.

"That's what the book's for," she said. "So the second one's got something to go on." She turned the pages back, carefully, past the map, past *the strut stood up* and *Toren, can't tell* and *Jab, unprompted, on a person*, past *Everybody inside it has to stand like me*, all the way back to the very front, to the first page, which Callie had seen her write on before anybody came, on the first morning, and cover with her hand.

She took her hand away.

There was one line on it. It was in Dessa's writing, very neat, in the middle of the page, with a great deal of white round it.

*Say it out loud. Even when it's awful.*

And under it, smaller: *M. F. On the wall.*

Callie looked at it for a long time. She did not know Milo. She knew his name, which Senna said every night at the tenth hour to a ward full of strangers, and which a four-year-old had started saying with her. She knew he had had a lamp, and that it had been terrible, and that it had got them somewhere. She knew there was a slab of bread every night at hut eleven folded in a cloth at the end of the row.

"He said it on the wall," said Dessa. "On the Level. When he was—" She stopped. She did not go on. She put her hand flat on the page, beside the line, not over it. "It's the only rule there is for a book like this. So it's the first thing in it."

"It's a good rule," said Callie.

"Senna says it's the best rule anybody ever gave her," said Dessa. "She says a boy on a wall made it up." She shut the book. "She's right. He did."

---

At the bottom of the page for the twenty-fifth day, below all the columns, there was one more line. Callie had not seen it until Dessa shut the book and she saw it upside down, for an instant, through the gap of the pages closing. It was not in Dessa's hand. It was Toren's, careful and slow, the way a boy writes with a stitched palm.

*Why (S. V., dictated): Because the ones who hold the line are the ones nobody watches.*

---

Doctor Sowerby told her mother the next morning, at the eighth hour, with his folder shut under his arm.

"I wanted a picture of your head," he said. "At four weeks. To see what the pills are doing to the swelling. I'd have liked it. I'd have learned something from it, about you and about the next person." He looked at the folder. "It won't happen. The machine's on cycles only, from today. Pictures only if somebody's dying, and then only if I think the picture would change what I do. Yours wouldn't. So you're not getting one."

"Is that the bad thing?" said her mother.

"That's the bad thing."

"What's the good thing?"

Sowerby looked at her for a moment.

"I'll look at your face instead," he said. "Every morning. And listen to her." He did not look at Callie. "She tells me exactly the right amount."

"Then look at it," said her mother, and put her chin up, and let him.

---

Toren told them on the steps that night.

He told them the grey-haired man had come up the slope to find him in the afternoon, which the grey-haired man never did, and had stood at the door of the shed with ink up the side of his hand and said that the box beside the fourth box, third row down, fifth along, had started to wake slow as well. Not as bad. A hair. But the same way.

"He said that's how it goes," said Toren. "One goes, and the ones either side work harder to agree with it, and then they go too." He was quiet. "He said four weeks was a week ago. It's less now. He wouldn't say how much less."

Nobody said anything.

The lamp at the end of the row was lit, flat and white, putting its circle down on the frozen concrete the colour of bone. Jab was on the step of twelve. He had made Otto bring him back from the long building at the fourth hour, against Sowerby, against Keel, against the woman with the good boots, who had said in front of everybody that he was an idiot and then helped Otto with the blankets. He sat on his step wrapped in both of them with his hands in his lap. They were still shaking. The long cord was not on his wrist. It was on Tonk's, in the fifth bed on the right, and the other end was still tied to the rail of the empty sixth bed, in a knot a four-year-old had made a nurse do twice, and Tonk had said nobody was to untie it.

Toren was on the step of eleven with his stitched hand on his knee.

Callie was on the step of fourteen with the hook across her knees and a finger of water in the well.

---

"Gran says Caul Hill," said Toren.

He said it to the lamp, not to either of them.

"She told me on the twenty-first. In the ward, at night. I wrote it down in Dessa's book because she asked me to." He did not look at them. "Six days north and west, on the old line. A hill with the top taken off and eleven miles of fence round it, and inside the fence, rooms of racks. With the same boxes in them. The same mark on the plates." He stopped. "She pulled the backs off them for the copper when she was fifty-three, and threw the boxes on the floor, because they were heavy and they were warm and the man who ran the crew said never to carry one. Hundreds. On the floor. Where they dropped them."

Callie did not say anything.

"She was in there eleven days," said Toren. "That's where her hands went. And her feet. That's eleven days."

"The Director said the old plant," said Callie. "On the second floor. The sky's gone wrong over it. Something opened there in the summer. There's ash lying in drifts on the ground that nobody's lifting, because nobody goes near."

"Same place," said Toren.

"Same place," said Callie.

---

Jab had not said anything. He sat on his step with his hands shaking in his lap and looked at the lamp.

"Dee had a Handful," he said, at last. "Settled. When she put her hand on the holder, beside mine. I felt it go into her. It wasn't Dee. It came out of her like it came out of nobody." He was quiet for a while. "It held the holder up while they got her on the stretcher and up the road to the lip. That's a quarter of a mile, near enough. And yesterday she did a quarter of a Handful into Rook's hands and stood exactly where she'd stood." He moved one of his hands, the way he moved them when he was doing a sum and running out of fingers. "A Handful for a quarter mile of holding somebody up. That's what it costs. I don't know what it costs for eleven days."

Callie looked at him.

"Nobody could go in there," she said. "If it took her hands in eleven days."

"No," said Jab. "Not on their own."

He did not say anything else. He did not need to. Callie heard the rest of it, the way she heard a husk line, not a track so much as a direction. Not on their own.
