Not without somebody holding them up the whole time they were in there. And not without something besides themselves to hold them up with. Jars. A lot of jars.

And ash in drifts on the ground up there, that nobody was lifting.

She looked at Toren. He was looking at the lamp. His face was doing nothing at all, which was how she knew.

---

None of them said it.

Callie heard all three of them not say it. She heard it the way she had heard the scan door not open, on the tenth day, and read a four-year-old lying still off the fact that nobody came out to fetch his brother. It was in what did not happen. Toren did not say *I'm going*. Jab did not say *you'd need somebody to hold you up*. She did not say *you'd need somebody to hold the line while you did it*. They sat on their three steps in the flat white light and did not say any of it, and it sat there between the three steps anyway, the size of a hill with the top taken off.

A part in a room full of racks. A tear in the sky above it. Ash on the ground there that nobody had lifted. A poison that took a woman's hands in eleven days. A machine with four weeks in it, and less since yesterday. A south lip at the end of the road with a hollow in the gravel the size of a saucer, and nobody standing in it tomorrow.

It was all one place.

She had not known that, this morning. She knew it now the way you know a thing about ground: not because anybody told you, but because you had been standing on it for a while and it had shown you.

---

The lamp went out at the first hour.

It did not flicker. It went, all at once, for its written reason, and the dark came down over the three steps and the row and the whole of Yard C.

Callie sat in it.

She could not see Toren now, or Jab, but she knew where they were. She could hear the stove tick through the door of eleven, and Jab's blankets move on twelve, and somebody's breathing, slow, which might have been either of them. She did not count it.

She went down into herself instead, the way she did every morning, and every night now, and looked. There was a finger of cold clear water in the bottom of the well, above the floor. She had kept it. She had stood in a ditch with nothing on her and kept it, and her mother had held her hand on the blanket and said *good*, and it was hers, and tomorrow there would be a little more.

Somewhere at the north end of the station, under a long low building with a turf roof, there was a pale ring standing on its edge in the dark, and a rack of little windows behind it, and two of them were yellow now.

Somewhere at the other end of a road there was a hollow in the gravel at the south lip of the Sag, filling up with frost, with nobody standing in it.

And somewhere six days north and west, on the old line, there was a hill with the top taken off, and a sky gone wrong above it, and rooms of racks going down under it in the dark with the backs pulled off, and on the floors of those rooms, where a crew had thrown them twenty years ago because they were heavy and warm and nobody should carry one, hundreds of grey boxes, lying where they fell.

Waiting, Callie thought, for somebody to count them.

---

## FORMULA ALIGNMENT — EDITORIAL, NOT BLIND

# O'Connor 1.3.0 — numerical author formula

# Applying the owner's numerical craft formula

The owner explicitly selected
`/Users/drive/penname/research/ironprince-craft-formula.md` to govern this book.
Use its craft instructions more or less exactly, including the numbers. These
are active design targets for the planning model and Opus/Fable author, not merely
editorial observations. The compiler embeds sections 2–6 and 8–9 verbatim below
these instructions and records the source hash so runs can be compared.

Let the sentence-length distribution, short/long sentence shares, paragraph
rhythm and Flesch reading targets influence the actual prose. Let the POV shares,
front-loaded progression vocabulary and distributed character-development rates
influence how the book is built. Preserve section 8's secondary/use-to-taste
status; do not turn its explicitly excluded filler/repetition tics into quotas.

Write original scenes in 6–8-chapter movements. The formula constrains the intended
shape and cadence; the author still chooses conversations, tactics, discoveries
and how events unfold. Do not reduce this brief to “clear writing with progression.”
The owner names the cast. No source novel quotations, character substitutions or
scene-by-scene replication belong in the manuscript.

Aim at the supplied distributions across a movement and the cumulative book.
Book-third teaching proportions and book-level POV proportions need not be met
independently by each chapter. Do not stop after each sentence or chapter to run a
gate. Write the full movement, then compare target versus observed results in the
existing editorial pass and make a focused same-author repair for material drift.
Do not silently broaden tolerances or drop inconvenient targets.

Keep the source's own caveats: pause-derived paragraph/scene measures are
directional proxies, and development counts are heuristic. Their adoption as
design targets does not establish that they accurately measure the printed book.
The full source-book audit remains unfinished. Keep source values such as ~1.7x
and ~950 words unchanged; the known arithmetic/denominator questions belong in
the audit, not a silent rewrite of the owner's chosen prompt. If competing targets
cannot be reconciled, report the conflict rather than fabricating a passing score.

This prompt guides output; it does not train model weights or guarantee exact
statistical compliance. Compliance must be measured after prose exists.

Source: /Users/drive/penname/research/ironprince-craft-formula.md
Source SHA-256: 9f97e2f225c0bcf61d2e922719153fcf00e3b029f7b1552f213888d33130c0b8

The following craft sections (2–6, 8–9) are included verbatim. Sections 1 and 7's legal discussion is not drafting guidance. Use original names, world, systems, events and phrasing; the owner names the cast.

## 2. Sentence rhythm

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Mean sentence length | 14.6 words |
| Median sentence length | 11 words |
| Spread (population stdev) | ~26 words — wide, meaning short declaratives sit right next to long compound-complex sentences, not clustered near the mean |
| Share of very short sentences (≤5 words) | 27.7% |
| Share of very long sentences (≥40 words) | 3.3% |

**Read:** roughly 1 sentence in 4 is a hard, short beat (often a single clause landing a
punch after a longer setup) — this is the "short sentence for impact" technique, not
uniform mid-length prose. Target this ratio, not just the mean; a manuscript that hits mean
14.6 but has no short-sentence spikes will not read the same.

## 3. Paragraph and scene rhythm (ASR-inferred proxy)

Whisper transcripts carry no typographic paragraph marks — this section is inferred from
pause gaps between narrated speech segments, not a count of the print manuscript's actual
paragraph breaks. Treat as directional, not exact.

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Inferred paragraph length (pause ≥0.6s) | median 18 words, mean 26.8 words — short, frequent beats |
| Inferred scene/section length (pause ≥1.6s) | mean ~950 words between scene-level breaks, ~8.7 scene breaks per 10k words |

**Read:** short paragraphs are the default texture, with scenes running roughly 900–1000
words before a beat change (POV settle, location change, or time-skip). Avoid long
unbroken expository blocks; break early and often.

## 4. Progression-mechanic ("change frequency") callout rate

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Progression/stat-vocabulary density | ~58 hits per 10k words (rank, growth-analog, spec, level-up, stat-block language) |
| Placement | front-loaded — the opening third of the book carries roughly 1.7x the progression-callout density of the middle and closing thirds (900 vs. 471 vs. 514 hits, by third) |

**Read:** the system gets *established* early and hard, then recedes into the background
as character and plot take over — it does not stay evenly dense through the whole book.
Build your own progression system's vocabulary (do not reuse his axis names — see §7), but
match this front-loaded shape: teach the mechanic aggressively in the first third, then let
it surface only at genuine turning points afterward.

## 5. Character-development beat cadence

Methodology: named-character mention within ~120 characters of a reflection/interiority
marker ("realized," "decided," "for the first time," "swore," "no longer," etc.) — a
heuristic count, not a scene-by-scene read.

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Protagonist development-moment rate | 4.5 per 10k words |
| Protagonist distribution across thirds | roughly even (41 / 56 / 50 — a slight middle-third bump, not backloaded) |
| Supporting-cast development-moment rate | an order of magnitude lower per character (0.1–0.7 per 10k words each), spread across 8–10 named characters rather than concentrated in one deuteragonist |

**Read:** growth beats for the lead land steadily throughout, not saved up for a finale —
and the supporting cast gets *many* small beats each rather than one character getting a
big secondary arc. If your story gives one best-friend character 80% of the secondary
development budget, that's a different shape than this book's.

## 6. POV structure

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Protagonist POV share | ~87% of chapter-word-volume (combining his given-name and surname references) |
| Secondary-character POV/focus share | ~13%, spread across 4–5 named characters at 2.5–3.7% each — brief cutaways, not sustained alternating-POV chapters |

**Read:** this is single-POV-dominant with occasional short cutaway scenes to a handful of
other named characters, not a true multi-POV structure. If you want to signal "feels like
Iron Prince," don't build alternating-chapter dual POV — keep the lead's POV overwhelming
and let other characters get glimpses, not chapters.

## 8. Supporting craft signals (secondary, use to taste)

- **Filler-word discipline:** "that" runs hot (92.1 per 10k words) — a known, common genre
  tic, not a target to imitate; if anything, tighten below this. Other fillers ("just,"
  "almost," "felt," "seemed") sit in the 5–25-per-10k band — moderate, not aggressively
  self-edited, not sloppy either.
- **Adverb density:** ~161 -ly adverbs per 10k words — fairly high; this is a book that
  lets adverbs carry tone rather than insisting on pure show-don't-tell. Not a craft flaw
  to correct, a genre-register signal to optionally match.
- **Dialogue-tag (reporting-verb) density:** ~41 per 10k words — moderate dialogue presence,
  consistent with an action/interiority-forward book rather than a dialogue-driven one.
- **Combat-vocabulary density:** ~22 per 10k words (strike/block/dodge/blade/impact/etc.) —
  present throughout, not just in set-piece fight chapters.
- **Readability:** Flesch Reading Ease 72.3, Flesch-Kincaid grade 6.8 — accessible YA/adult
  crossover register, short-to-medium sentence construction, low jargon-density outside the
  progression-vocabulary spikes.
- **Repetition discipline:** common connective 3-grams ("out of the," "one of the," "for a
  moment") sit at 2–4 per 10k words — normal English-prose background rate, not a
  distinctive tic to replicate. Don't chase these; they're noise, not signature.

---

## 9. One-paragraph brief for the drafting agent

> Write single-POV-dominant (~85–90% lead-character screen time), short-paragraph,
> pause-heavy prose (median paragraph ~18 words, scenes breaking roughly every 900–1000
> words). Mix short punch sentences (~28% of all sentences ≤5 words) against longer
> compound-complex ones (mean 14.6 words, wide spread) rather than settling into uniform
> mid-length sentences. Establish your progression system hard and often in the first
> third of the book, then let it recede to occasional high-stakes surfacing afterward. Land
> a protagonist growth/realization beat roughly every 2,200 words, spread evenly across the
> book rather than saved for the climax, and give 8–10 supporting characters small
> development beats each rather than concentrating growth in one deuteragonist. Moderate
> dialogue-tag and adverb use; accessible ~7th-grade reading level; combat vocabulary
> present in the background throughout, not confined to fight scenes. Every name, every
> system-label, every scene: yours, invented, original.

## NUMERICAL REVIEW

# Numerical formula alignment — after the movement

This is an informed editorial pass, not a blind cold read. Read the supplied
formula and full movement, then report target, observed result, method, coverage
and uncertainty. Keep numerical alignment separate from reader scores.

- Sentence mean, median, population standard deviation; shares ≤5 and ≥40 words.
- Written paragraph mean/median and actual scene/section boundaries. Compare with
  the requested targets, but do not equate typography with ASR pause segmentation.
- Flesch Reading Ease and Flesch-Kincaid grade. Identify the sentence tokenizer and
  syllable estimator; proper names/system terms can affect estimates.
- Progression vocabulary hits per 10,000 words, and placement by equal word-count
  book thirds once available. Define a project-specific lexicon before counting;
  do not borrow the source novel's names. An incomplete book cannot confirm the
  full front-loading ratio. Report provisional cumulative coverage instead.
- Lead development moments and supporting-cast distribution: cite actual choices
  or realizations and distinguish manually identified beats from regex proxies.
- POV word share: identify viewpoint segments, not the most frequently mentioned
  name. Track the lead and secondary viewpoints against the book-level allocation.
- Secondary signals: -ly words, reporting verbs, combat terms, fillers and repeated
  phrases. Publish lexicons and counting definitions. A suffix count is not proof
  that every matched word is an adverb. Preserve optional/non-target distinctions.

Use reproducible counts when tooling exists. Otherwise mark a metric UNMEASURED;
never estimate a precise score by intuition. Save the method or command with the
report so later runs use the same definitions. Inspect ASR reference limitations
without discarding the owner's chosen drafting targets.

Locate meaningful departures, especially uniform sentence lengths, missing short
beats, reading level drift, dense paragraphs, late teaching or a coequal second
lead replacing the requested POV shape. Consolidate the most important repairs
with continuity findings into the existing bounded author repair. Protect exact
owner text and successful scenes. Do not iterate indefinitely to hit decimals or
hide irreconcilable targets. Bring consequential formula conflicts to the owner.

---

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — BOOK_MAP.md

# Book Two: Meridian — living book map

Status: revised after owner ruling 2026-09-27. Material labeled `OWNER` is now
locked direction for Book Two; everything else remains `POSSIBLE` unless marked
`CANON`. Where this map draws on locked canon (`CANON.md`, `UNIVERSE_BIBLE.md`,
`NAME_REGISTRY.md`, the Ch. 27–28 / 18–20 / 11–14 source pool), that material is
cited, not altered.

Prepared by the planning model (Sonnet 5), per the owner's brief. Series title
**Kindled**; this book's working title **Meridian**.

---

## 0. A conflict this map has to name before anything else

Two prior authoring sessions already produced full Book Two architectures:
`books/book-01-hobbs-wall/BOOK_2_MAP.md` ("The Ledger") and
`books/book-01-tallow-gate/BOOK_MAP_2.md` (untitled). Both are well built and
both share the same load-bearing assumption: **the protagonist set is Toren,
Dessa, and Wyck**, with Rook and Senna as the adult/elder pressure, and Callie
and Jab held at the edge of the frame as neighbors whose material is "kept,
but compressed to a single movement's complication."

That assumption conflicts directly with the owner's ruling for *this* book:
Callie, Jab, and Toren are **all three** protagonists, none demotable. The two
existing drafts are not wrong — they are answers to a different brief, written
before this one existed. Their actual content is largely reusable:

- **Fully reusable as locked mechanism**, independent of which characters carry
  it: Guard as Stride's second shape (`CANON.md`, cross-confirmed by both
  drafts already), the Sag line and ash-lifting economy, the medicine/residency
  gate split, Director's-office-style institutional pressure, the "no
  power inside the wall" no-power-inside-the-line convention*, and the
  general engine correction ("growth, powers, and fights are the spine;
  institutional pressure is load underneath it, not the plot").
- **Not directly portable**: both drafts' *character* engine — Toren, Dessa,
  and Wyck each privately believing their door is capped, and each breaking
  that belief across three call-outs — is built for a five-person, single-lead-
  adjacent cast. It cannot be lifted wholesale into a book that owes Callie and
  Jab full arcs, or Dessa and Wyck end up re-absorbing the book's whole growth
  budget again, exactly the imbalance the owner is correcting.

  <sub>*The Hobb's-Wall draft's "no worked power inside the wall" rule does not
  appear in `CANON.md` as locked — it is that draft's own invention to force
  growth outward onto call-outs. This map does not adopt it as written; see
  §11, continuity risks.</sub>

**This map's resolution:** keep the mechanism (Guard, ash-lifting, the Sag,
the Director's-office pressure, the "discovery under real pressure, not a
workshop" rule), discard the character engine, and rebuild the growth engine
across three protagonists plus a defender cohort. The owner then joined the
two proposed outward objectives into one load-bearing mission: the team must
recover the failing treatment-machine part at Caul Hill **and** reach and
secure the new ash-rich rip there. The large ash reserve is not bonus loot; it
is the fuel that lets Rook, Jab, and **Dee Wren**, the canon Homura survivor,
recall the
radiation burden from exposed bodies long enough for the party to enter, work,
fight, and come back alive. `OWNER`.

---

## 1. Verdict: the strongest Book Two engine

**The team survived the road. Meridian is where they find out that surviving
it wasn't the same as being safe — and that the only place any of them
actually gets better is back outside the wall, doing the work Meridian cannot
spare enough qualified adults to do.** Concretely: Meridian's Sag line is
worsening faster than its registered defenders can cover; a new and more
dangerous rip has opened near Caul Hill; and the one machine that keeps Senna's
marrow-support course calibrated—and gives Meridian its only advanced imaging
for cases such as Tilda's and Tonk's—needs a part from the irradiated plant
beside it. Reaching the part and securing the rip are one mission. The team must carry large stores of
graded ash because Sustain can recall active radiation from a living body only
while someone keeps paying for the work. That necessity turns three separated
arrivals into one company: Callie, Jab, and Toren go with Rook and Dee Wren,
while their coaches and cohort make the expedition possible.
Training, combat, ash economics, and found-family bonding all happen because
the mission must be prepared for, survived, and paid for. Institutional
politics supplies pressure and consequence. It does not replace the adventure.

## 2. Book promise, emotional promise, progression promise, irreversible ending change

- **Book promise:** the three roads that became one group at Meridian's gate
  become one working company by this book's end — not by declaration, by
  having solved a problem together that none of their three separate roads
  could have solved alone.
- **Emotional promise:** each protagonist discovers that the cost of the road
  didn't end at the gate. Callie's counting, Jab's caregiving arithmetic, and
  Toren's leadership weight all find a new, harder object — a mother who
  won't let her count, a brother who will need him for thirteen years, a
  grandmother whose hands are already going — and each has to grow a
  different kind of strength to carry it that isn't the strength that got
  them here.
- **Progression promise:** all three protagonists cross a real threshold
  under real stakes, never merely in a drill: Toren gets Guard to hold on
  purpose; Callie's twin-kindled Ward (Edge and Ward together,
  `UNIVERSE_BIBLE.md`) is tested at group scale; and Jab learns that his
  "second thing" is Sustain — sensing, supporting a failing body, and, with
  ash, recalling active radiation from tissue. Jab's rare fit for the Caul
  Hill run makes him Meridian's primary recruit, which gives his growth a
  human cost: the station first sees a scarce medical asset where Tonk sees
  his brother. None of the three becomes what the adults already are. They
  become people whose separate capabilities can keep one another alive.
- **Irreversible ending change:** the ceiling every one of them believed in —
  Callie's "I only read ground," Jab's "it doesn't come when I call it,"
  Toren's "it's out and I don't know when it's coming back" — turns out to
  have been an untested assumption, not a fact. That, not the accord with the
  Director's office, is the book's real irreversible change. Layered under it:
  a named household's residency injustice gets fixed, at real cost, using the
  team's new capability as the actual instrument of the fix; Meridian's only
  advanced treatment/imaging machine is repaired without magically curing any
  patient; and Cinder Company's
  remnant is confirmed re-forming under a new name, off-page, opening Book
  Three's canvas. The north yard also remains after the expedition as a real
  teaching place—with separate practice lanes and records—planting the
  institutional home of the future 2680 story.

## 3. The central outward mission

### One mission: the part and the rip (`OWNER`)

The former Options A and B are now one operation. Senna's treatment shed runs
on a single, pre-Fall machine at least seventy years old (`CANON.md`, Ch. 27–28), and a part in its
calibration train is failing. The only known compatible salvage is at Caul Hill
— the irradiated pre-Fall plant where Senna received her original exposure.
A new and more dangerous rip has opened at or beneath the same complex. It has
made the site harder to enter, but it has also left the one resource the team
cannot make the attempt without: deep stores of ownerless reserve-ash.

The objectives cannot be separated:

1. Carry enough clean, graded ash from Meridian to fund the approach and the
   first radiation-recall cycles. The team cannot count on ash it has not yet
   won.
2. Cross the contaminated approach while Rook, Jab, and Dee Wren
   rotate Sustain duty, recalling active radiological burden before it fixes
   into lasting tissue damage.
3. Reach the calibration assembly and recover the compatible part.
4. Contain or collapse the new rip, kill its new Riftspawn expression through
   a full-team action, and recover the resulting ash so the healers can bring
   everyone home.

The third former option — making the disk mystery the journey's reason — stays
retired. The disk receives one costly clue late in the book, not the book's
engine.

### Why it must leave Meridian

The shed's machine cannot be repaired from spare parts on hand; Meridian's own
hardened depot inventory has nothing compatible (a callback to Annice Fell's
ledger, Ch. 28: things go into that depot, they do not come out). The active
rip also cannot be ignored: if it grows, it changes the Sag from a strained
defense line into an open road for stronger Riftspawn. Caul Hill is roughly a
week's travel past the Sag's worst stretch — close enough to reach inside the
machine's failure clock, far enough to be a true expedition.

### Why adults cannot simply solve it

Two separate, compounding reasons, both institutional rather than a plot
contrivance:

1. **Every ordinary defender who could lead it is already spoken for.** Meridian's
   registered defenders — the people who can actually hold a road shut —
   are stretched thin covering the worsening Sag line itself (this is the
   book's own opening pressure). Pulling one of them off the wall for a
   week to escort a salvage run means a week of the Sag going unheld, which
   the Director's office will not authorize under any argument.
2. **The site selects for a very rare team, not for youth.** Caul Hill's
   radiation cannot be endured by general toughness or fast healing. Sustain
   can recall the *active burden* from tissue, but it cannot undo old damage
   after that damage has fixed. Rook and Jab can do the work; the Homura
   cross-medic understands how to direct it, rotate healers, and increase the
   amount of ash a Kindled soul can safely hold and spend. Callie and Toren
   supply the Ward, Guard, and extraction capability that keep those healers
   alive. The party is selected by complementary doors and training, not by
   the premise that children tolerate radiation better than adults.

### What failure costs

If the part is not recovered, Senna's and (per the same shed, same cycle)
every other patient's course begins to fail within roughly eleven days — the
same interval already established as the treatment's own clock (Ch. 27–28).
Failure is not abstract: it is a countable number of cycles before Senna's
marrow support lapses, stated on the page the way Pell states a distance.
Secondary cost: if the mission is caught leaving without authorization, the
whole clan's residency review is jeopardized, which threatens every household
riding on it (Callie's, Jab's, the Hobb's Wall clan's).

### How it joins the Sag / residency / ash / defender threads

The mission is staffed, provisioned, and made survivable using everything the
book's other threads have taught. The team carries large stores of lifted,
graded ash as fuel, not as trade cargo and not as a lucky find. Toren's Guard
and Callie's widened Ward protect the Sustain rotation. Jab senses changes in
the bodies under his care and learns to recall radiation deliberately. Rook
teaches him the lived technique; Dee teaches the repeatable
capacity discipline: take ash in, settle it without confusing it for personal
reserve, spend it through Sustain, vent the harmful load, and refill before
the soul bottoms out. The defender cohort covers the gap at Meridian while
the expedition is away. The ash secured at the rip then becomes the material
proof that the team can protect Meridian and alter the exploitative residency
economy, rather than simply argue against it.

## 4. Three-protagonist arc table

| | **Callie** | **Jab** | **Toren** |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Want** | To keep her mother alive by doing the one thing she's good at — reading, counting, solving | To be enough for Tonk for the thirteen years the leg will cost, without the road's excuse of urgency to hide behind | To find out whether the towpath breakthrough (Ch. 26, prior book) was a door or a fluke, and to be worth what Meridian's card already says he is |
| **False limit** | Believes her Ward is a personal shield only — something that protects one person (herself, at most one other) and nothing larger | Believes his "second thing" is unreliable crisis instinct and that caring for Tonk is the only legitimate use he can make of it | Believes Edge+Stride was an adrenaline accident, not a repeatable skill — and that "one shape, out at present" means he may have less than he started with |
| **Learned capability** | Holds a shared Ward at scale for the first time — over more than herself, under real time pressure, across the Caul Hill extraction lane | Learns that sensing and healing are one Sustain domain; deliberately recalls active radiation from another body, uses stored ash to keep doing it, and identifies the Riftspawn's renewal pulse before it can rebuild | Reaches Guard on purpose: Edge for offense, Stride locked rigid for defense, blended and reproducible, then uses it as the fixed point the whole extraction rotates around |
| **Relational change** | Lets her mother's "you don't get to spend yourself on me" become something she can actually live inside, instead of a rule she resents; becomes the one Toren and Jab both trust with the worst news first | Stops being only Tonk's carrier and becomes a healer the entire team relies on, while refusing Meridian's attempt to value him only as a recruitable medical asset | Takes responsibility for a mixed team whose survival depends on Callie's and Jab's different strengths, rather than treating leadership as doing Rook's job younger |
| **Costly choice** | Demonstrates and then spends her Ward for the whole team even though every added body shortens the hold | Accepts the primary-recruit training because it is the only route to mastering radiation recall, but sets Tonk and his household—not Meridian's labor claim—as the boundary on that service | Leads the tactical extraction through the rip zone, staking his judgment on the ash budget and Sustain rotation instead of on personal force |
| **Ending proof** | Holds a Ward wide enough to keep the Sustain lane intact during the team fight | Recalls radiation on purpose, rotates out before overdraw, refills from ash, returns to the line, and brings another person out clean enough to recover | Guard remains fixed while the new Riftspawn changes tactics, giving Callie and Jab the stable seconds needed to finish their parts |

### The people they came to save: medical and injury progression (`OWNER`)

The patients are not a reason to leave in Movement 3 and a reward waiting in
Movement 6. Their conditions move through the whole book. Every return to the
treatment shed or huts must show one concrete change: a better morning, a failed
movement, a fever, a medicine cost, a rehabilitation gain, or a new limit. The
Caul Hill clock is therefore visible in people rather than represented only by a
number on a machine.

| Person | Book 2 opening state | Required progression before departure | Departure heart-string | End-of-book state |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Tilda** | The growth in the left side of her head is inoperable and cannot be shrunk. Pills can reduce swelling and headaches; a second medicine may suppress the fits. Her right hand and leg do not reliably take instruction, and her words sometimes fail. She has refused the prognosis number. | The pills give her back some clear mornings without curing the growth. Improvement must be uneven: one recovered word or easier morning beside one fit, failed step, or loss of the right hand. Callie learns to report what happened without converting her mother into a countdown. | Tilda has a bad morning close enough to departure that Callie means to stay. Tilda, fully lucid, refuses to become the reason Callie abandons the only mission that can keep the shed working. Her instruction is not “save me”; it is the Book One lesson returned: stop holding so hard and let what has been built hold. | Alive, improved in comfort rather than cured. The repaired machine protects the care around her; it does not repair the growth. She keeps authority over whether the prognosis number is ever spoken. |
| **Tonk** | The dead bone has been removed and the leg reset with four pins and an external bar. The infection has a four-in-five chance of clearing after the first operation; the answer comes at roughly two weeks. The frame stays until spring. The damaged growth plate means a likely limp, a built-up boot, annual measurement, and probable later surgery. | Fever and wound checks carry the first medical clock. He must have at least one setback before the infection result and at least one small rehabilitation victory after it: eating, sitting, transferring to a chair, or moving himself without Jab doing it for him. He remains four, funny, angry, and frightened—not a solemn symbol. | Tonk initially assumes the carrying frame means he can go. The answer is medically no. Jab has not chosen to be beyond Tonk's reach since the accident, so leaving is harder than the road ever was. Use their existing cord and wooden fish: Tonk is the one who releases Jab's end and gives him something to bring back, reversing the six-week pattern in which Jab never let go. | Infection controlled if the first operation succeeds, but the frame and long future remain. Jab returns to a brother who has progressed without him; that is relief and a wound, not proof Jab was unnecessary. |
| **Senna** | The active radiation course takes about twenty weeks; marrow support repeats every eleven days for life. The course is already taking sensation from hands and feet and will likely take walking by spring. She will never travel again. | Each movement marks the neuropathy honestly: cup, button, step, chair, then work she can still do. Her increasing physical dependence is paired with increasing strategic usefulness. She identifies Caul Hill from memory and helps build the route/exposure ledger without being reduced to exposition. | Caul Hill is where the injury that has governed twenty years of her life began, and she is the one person who cannot return. She sends Toren with a route detail or object only she could supply, then makes the whole room acknowledge that this is the last road she can help plan but not walk. | Course continues. Some active burden may be reduced, but fixed marrow and nerve damage remain. Her new role is teacher, recorder, and institutional memory of the yard. |
| **Rook** | Healing puncture through the back; recently unable to stand for more than minutes or hold a road again for days. He has also signed four seasons of the worst north-yard work. | Ability returns by measured functions, not a reset: sitting and rising, one short hold, one full rotation, then paired Sustain work. The scars and reduced margin still affect his Caul Hill ash budget. | He goes because the recall lane needs him, while knowing Senna cannot follow him onto the place that injured her. Their goodbye should stay practical until one concrete Caul Hill detail makes the history personal. | Functional, not untouched. He ends capable of teaching and field work, with the debt and physical cost still present. |
| **Dessa** | Chronic bad leg aggravated by the last approach; compensates with precision and her thirty-foot awareness. | She is the cohort's best observer and injury logger. The leg limits distance but not authority; she discovers that training design is a form of defense. | She stays to coordinate Meridian's yard/Sag defense and watch Tonk, Tilda, and Senna. This is not being left behind; it is command of the home line. | The leg remains. Her methods become the first repeatable records the future yard can inherit. |
| **Wyck** | Broken collarbone and wrist, numb fingers, newly using a replacement bar one-handed. | Rehabilitation is paired with Resilience training: pain tolerance is not healing, and immovability can worsen an injury if used stupidly. He learns to stop before damage while helping design safe contact drills. | He stays on the home defense because he cannot yet carry or strike safely for a week-long mission. It should hurt his pride and then become an earned choice to hold what the travelers are leaving. | Bone healing is real but incomplete. His distinct body-discipline lane has a named place in the yard. |
| **Toren and Callie** | Toren enters with a broken left forearm, opened right palm, and stitched leg; Callie with healing ribs and head stitches after the scarp. | Their training load rises only as the injuries permit. Both lose sessions by being honest about pain, preventing progression from reading as consequence-free. | They are cleared for the mission because they recover enough and because their abilities are indispensable, not because wounds disappear between books. | Cleared for full work with scars and learned limits carried forward. |

**Departure scene architecture:** intercut the two hardest releases. Tonk wants
to go with Jab and has to choose to let him leave; Callie decides she cannot go
after Tilda's bad morning, and Tilda chooses for herself that Callie will. Toren
watches both and understands that leading this company means returning three
people to the beds and rooms that made them leave. Do not kill a patient merely
to manufacture permission for the mission. The struggle to go is the cost; an
eventual death must be earned by the individual medical arc, not added as a
launch mechanism.

### Same door, different hands: the Ember comparison curriculum (`OWNER`)

The north yard becomes compelling when it stops asking whether someone “has
Edge” and starts comparing what Edge actually does in different lives. The
three protagonists should train together often enough that the reader can see
the rules through contrast rather than explanation.

| Practitioner | Existing expression | What the comparison reveals | Book 2 progression |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Jab** | One mature fish-knife shape: heavy belly, hooked tip. He can hold it all day and deliberately kindle its edge-light when he needs it sharp; the light reliably catches at the belly. | Jab is not summoning a separate knife spell. His Ember answers with one deeply practised metal form, and Edge's second coupled act is the line of light along that form's working edge. Four years of tool use gave him control Toren lacks, even before combat training. | Learn to run the light cleanly from belly through hook, leave it dark for contact practice, and choose how much working edge a task needs. His advanced control is continuity and economy, not a sudden new weapon menu. |
| **Toren** | An ugly, crooked spike plus Stride struts; he has lit the spike hard under pressure and once held Edge and Stride together. | Yes, Toren can learn deliberate edge-light because it is native to Edge. No, he should not copy Jab's fish knife. His lesson is to light the spike he actually has, keep the line steady through impact, and stop mistaking another person's mature shape for his next rank. | Jab can teach the light better than Rook because he has lived inside one shape for four years. Toren's larger breakthrough remains Stride-as-Guard and a repeatable Edge+Stride blend. |
| **Callie** | Her inherited hook can carry both of her open doors: Ward makes it an immovable anchor; Edge produces a long, ugly curved sweep. She can put the effect in herself or the hook, but not yet everywhere at once. Attempts to force a shorter, straighter blade extinguish it. | Callie already *can* form a blade. The error is calling it the wrong blade because it does not look like Katori's. Her curve follows the hook, leverage, and the way she reads load. Ward wants to plant and distribute force; Edge wants to find and divide it. | First refine the curve she already owns—clean start, controlled length, dark training form, lit working edge—then learn a deliberate handoff between anchored Ward and cutting Edge. A wholly new straight blade stays rank-gated rather than arriving because someone explains it. |

The first recurring drill is one obstacle, three answers: Jab cuts a loaded
line at the exact point that saves the rig; Toren opens or holds the approach;
Callie anchors the load and then finds its failure line. Rook makes them swap
*problems*, not shapes. The lesson is that a common power system produces
personal techniques shaped by work, body, grief, and practice.

### The yard that becomes the schools (`OWNER` / future seed)

Meridian is the physical home of the future 2680 story. Book Two plants that
future without turning the present-day cast into an academy imitation:

- The **north yard** begins as a few marked lanes, borrowed equipment, and a
  shared ledger because Meridian needs defenders now. It is not chartered and
  does not yet call itself a school.
- Rook's mixed-door teaching becomes the yard's central principle: everyone
  learns the same problem, then solves it through the door and shape they
  actually possess. Toren records the combined-field method rather than being
  crowned its founder in advance.
- Wyck's safe Resilience/contact practice develops beside it as a distinct
  body-discipline lane. Dee's Ward/Sustain and ash-capacity work
  becomes the medical lane. Rift-response rotations remain a field tradition,
  not a classroom.
- Dessa starts the first append-only training ledger: exercise, observed
  failure, injury, reserve spent, recovery time, second attempt. Senna records
  oral histories and the reasons behind the drills. Those two acts are the
  believable seed of an institution surviving its founders.
- Across the next 530 years, those lanes may become the future Touren lineage,
  Wyck's school, and the independent rift-fighter tradition described in the
  2680 proposal. Those later names and exact institutional histories remain
  provisional until the owner locks them; Book Two only needs the yard, the
  methods, and records that can genuinely grow into them.

## 5. Mentor/teacher table

| Teacher | Domain taught | Method | Where it disagrees with the others | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Rook / Elias Marsh** | Edge, Stride, Ward, Sustain, and the Path in general | Teaches Jab radiation recall by feel and consequence: read the body, draw only what is still active, stop before the healer begins taking the burden personally, and use ash before personal reserve | Distrusts drilling for its own sake; knows the technique but not the complete Homura capacity method, which gives him a real reason to learn beside Jab | `CANON` (method and Sustain), radiation-recall role `OWNER` |
| **Katori** | Field/combat judgment, reading terrain and people under pressure | Teaches by trusting a student with real responsibility before they feel ready, then debriefing hard afterward | Clashes with Pell's slower, written-record discipline; also tests Dee Wren's reserve-safety doctrine, since Katori's judgment calls sometimes require spending past the safe line on purpose | `CANON` (established, Book 1), Book 2 role `POSSIBLE` |
| **Pell** | Ground-reading, cartographic/logistics discipline, the arithmetic of a road | Teaches by writing everything down and making a student defend the number, not the feeling | The most cautious of the group's own teachers; likely to be the one voice arguing against the Caul Hill mission's risk math, which gives the mission's departure real friction instead of easy consensus | `CANON` (established, Book 1), Book 2 role `POSSIBLE` |
| **The Elder (carried teaching)** | Practical craft and communal ethic — the valley's whole model of shared obligation, which is where Jab's caregiving logic comes from | Not present in person; **her** teaching is inherited and re-applied by Jab under new pressure, and should deepen through contact with Meridian's found-family rather than be replaced by it | The gentlest and least institutional of the teaching traditions; her ethic is what lets Jab resist becoming Meridian property when recruitment begins | `CANON` (Book 1 source pool), Book 2 deepening `POSSIBLE` |
| **Dee Wren** | Cross-tradition medicine, Sustain, access/control across multiple doors, and safe ash capacity | The Homura survivor whom Rook walked to safety teaches the part neither Rook nor Meridian's labor system knows how to formalize: increasing the soul's *usable working depth* so a Kindled person can absorb, settle, hold, spend, vent, and replenish more ash without confusing capacity with rank. Dee's drills are controlled; the field method is rotation, measurement, and stopping before a healer becomes the next patient | Dee respects Rook's instincts but rejects his willingness to discover limits in crisis. Rook knows how to save the person in front of him; Dee knows how to keep three healers functional through a six-hour contaminated operation. Their disagreement produces a better mission protocol, not a winner | Name and Homura survival `CANON`; cross-medical teaching role `OWNER`; age and exact door count remain undisclosed |

**Where the disagreement should surface on the page:** during preparation,
Rook wants Jab trained against a real body because Sustain is relational and
cannot be learned from an empty cot. Dee insists on ash-budgeted
cycles, a hard rotation, and an abort line. The mission proves both are right:
Jab needs Rook's feel to find the burden, and the medic's discipline to come
back from finding it.

## 6. Youth-defender cohort design

- **Purpose:** the Sag is worsening faster than Meridian's adult defenders
  can cover, and the station's own liability doctrine (registered Kindled
  residents are sorted into labor by door) already pulls Kindled teenagers
  toward dangerous work informally. The cohort formalizes that pull into
  something with adult oversight, rather than pretending it isn't happening.
- **Membership logic:** not "every Kindled kid at Meridian" — a small,
  specific group built from need and consent: Callie, Jab (if his household's
  situation allows; his instinct to keep working through the Elder's ethic
  makes this an easy yes for him and a harder one for the book to justify
  given Tonk), Toren, plus willing members of the Hobb's Wall clan (Dessa,
  Wyck) and at least one Meridian-native trainee to keep the cohort from
  reading as "the Book One cast plus scenery." Recommend one new named
  Meridian local, invented at drafting, who has no Book One history and
  therefore reacts to everything (the ash economy, the found-family's
  closeness, Rook's method) the way a reader coming in cold might.
- **Adult safeguards:** Dee Wren oversees formal reserve and Sustain drills; Rook
  oversees live Sag-line rotations; no cohort member holds a line alone
  without an adult in immediate support distance; injuries and overdraws are
  logged and reviewed, mirroring the station's own paperwork culture (a
  believable, in-world safeguard rather than an authorial promise the plot
  ignores).
- **Training progression:** shadowing → supervised jar-and-sack work behind
  an adult's held line → controlled short holds under direct supervision →
  full rotations with an adult in support range → the Caul Hill mission as
  the first expedition beyond Meridian's support perimeter. It is not an
  unsupervised youth test: Rook and Dee travel, while Katori and
  Pell divide field/navigation coaching and the defense of the people left
  behind.
- **Moral pressure:** the book should not resolve whether this is right. The
  Director's office is correct that the Sag needs more hands than it has;
  Pell and Senna's mother-thread (Callie's own mother's "you don't get to
  spend yourself on me") are correct that this trades children's bodies for
  a shortfall adults created. Both positions stay true at once. The named
  residency-injustice case (§3, §11) should be a cohort member's family —
  someone who took the ash-lifting-equivalent contract without a found
  family able to absorb the cost, exactly as both prior drafts already
  recommend — so the moral weight lands on a specific person, not a policy
  debate.
- **The first school without calling it one:** no uniforms, no ranked class
  structure, no shared dormitory-and-bell-schedule texture. Training happens
  in the same huts, the same stove-side conversations, and the same north
  yard the rest of the book already lives in. What *does* begin here is
  durable: three marked practice lanes, a shared equipment rack, and Dessa's
  append-only record of drills, injuries, reserve, recovery, and repeatable
  results. Every cohort member keeps a private want unrelated to combat
  (Callie's counting, Jab's caregiving, Toren's leadership anxiety, and
  whatever the new local recruit wants that has nothing to do with the Sag).

## 7. Ash-learning ladder

Grounded directly in the drafted Rook ash-collection scene
(`book-01-tallow-gate/book-02-drafts/movement-01-ash-collection.md`) and the
Ch. 27–28 / Ch. 18 "the jar" material — this ladder should be taught through
exactly that kind of scene, not through explanation.

1. **Recognition and collection.** Reading a drift's Yield by eye and feel
   before touching it (Flask vs. Handful, "a fair one" vs. "a dirty one," per
   the ash-collection draft); the flat-palm technique, working a span at a
   time, holding it low and open rather than closed. Failure mode: hauling —
   trying to pull ash faster than it comes, which yields less, not more.
   Teaching scene: Rook's drafted scene itself, adapted to include a cohort
   member learning alongside Wyck and Dessa rather than only overheard.
2. **Grading.** Handful / Flask / Barrel / Well (`CANON.md`, Yield) as a
   trade vocabulary, learned first as an insult-proof way to size up danger
   ("Handful or Flask?"), then as the literal unit the weighbridge pays in.
   Teaching scene: a weighbridge dispute (Ch. 18's "that's thinner than it
   ought to come" beat is the model) where a cohort member's read is
   challenged and has to be defended with evidence, not confidence.
3. **Cleaning and contamination.** Silt, ditch-water, and "a dirty Handful"
   as concrete hazards — contaminated ash burns unreliably and is priced
   down, or in the worst case damages whoever spends it (a training-safe
   version of an overdraw, introduced here in miniature). Teaching scene: a
   cohort member's first real weighbridge failure, graded down publicly, and
   having to learn why rather than being told the rule.
4. **Storage and carriage.** Jars, sacks, flasks, folded canvas between them
   (all attested in Ch. 18, 27, and the ash-collection draft); why quantity
   and grade both degrade under bad handling, and why a station's economy
   treats a jar of clean Flask as worth defending on the road.
5. **Taking it in — ash held by the soul.** Ash is ownerless, unlike a
   person's expended reserve. Skin-contact absorption is deliberate and
   unhurried: the same flat-palm stillness used to collect it, turned inward.
   Ash does not become a second organ or named storage tank; the soul holds
   it as usable reserve. Rushing intake causes spill, nausea, false fullness,
   or a lock that prevents clean spending.
6. **Increasing usable working depth.** Dee teaches that a
   Kindled soul does not become metaphysically larger in a week; the person
   learns to use more of the depth already present. Intake, settle, spend,
   vent, rest, repeat. Capacity is measured by clean cycles and recovery, not
   by how much ash someone can force in once. This is the training that makes
   the Caul Hill reserve practical instead of merely heavy cargo.
7. **Radiation recall through Sustain.** Rook teaches Jab to feel the active
   harmful burden in a body without confusing it with old injury. Sustain
   draws or recalls that burden while ash pays for the work. The technique
   prevents further damage during exposure; it does not erase damage already
   fixed in tissue and therefore does not retroactively cure Senna. The
   healer must vent the recalled burden and rotate out before it begins to
   lodge in the healer instead.
8. **Spending through a door under pressure.** Absorbed ash can power Edge,
   Stride, Ward, Sustain, or Resilience without first emptying personal
   reserve. Teaching scene: a controlled Sag-line failure where ash-fuel,
   not personal endurance, is the difference between holding and breaking.
9. **Advanced team use and overdraw.** The expedition uses a measured
   rotation rather than a vague shared pool: one person works, one holds the
   reserve, one watches for spill and overdraw. Spending past safe working
   depth causes visible damage even when ash remains available. This is why
   the party needs stores, trained bodies, and discipline rather than one
   miraculous healer.

## 8. Six movements

Chapter counts are planning ranges, not compulsory cards, per the owner's
direction to draft in 6–8-chapter runs later.

| Movement | Chapters | Dominant POV balance | Entry state | Training/progression | Human pressure | Action set piece | Turn | Carried injuries/resources | Exit state |
|---|---:|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **1 — Three Roads, One Wall** | 8 | Even rotation, Callie → Jab → Toren, redrafted from the Ch. 27–28/18–20/11–14 source pool as one shared opening | The joined rescue is just over; all three households are inside the gate, conditional, unreviewed | Cards, declarations, first ash-lifting exposure (the Rook ash-collection scene lands here); no deliberate technique training yet | Diagnoses land for Senna, Tilda, and Tonk in the same movement, cross-cut for the first time instead of stacked; grief for Milo; Rook's and Katori's Satori recognition | None major — this movement's "action" is institutional and medical, matching the source material's own register | The shared treatment shed is revealed to be single-point-of-failure infrastructure (a line added, not invented, extending Doctor Sowerby's "one machine... I don't know of another within four hundred miles") | Establish every medical baseline in §4: Tilda's palliative medicines, Tonk's infection clock/frame, Senna's eleven-day cycle and nerve loss, plus the returning party's injuries; everyone also has a residency clock | All three households conditionally admitted; no training begun; the machine's fragility is known only to the reader and a few adults |
| **2 — The North Yard** | 8 | Toren-leaning, with Callie and Jab both present and load-bearing | Cohort forms; Dee Wren recognizes Jab's second door and Meridian marks him as a primary medical recruit | Ash ladder stages 1–4; the first same-obstacle/three-answers drill contrasts Jab's lit fish knife, Toren's ugly spike, and Callie's hooked Ward/Edge; Toren's first failed deliberate Guard; Callie's first supervised wider Ward; Jab learns that sensing and healing are both Sustain | Recruitment threatens to turn Jab's gift into another debt contract; Katori, Pell, Rook, and Dee disagree about safe training | **Sag-line failure** (action sequence #1): a supervised hold breaks; Jab senses the injury before he sees it and stabilizes the casualty, but empties himself doing it | Rook identifies the instinct; Dee gives it a name and demonstrates that ash, correctly held, can fund it | Tonk's fever/infection answer approaches; Tilda has an uneven response to medicine; Senna loses more sensation; Wyck and Rook learn that grit cannot substitute for healing | The mission becomes necessary; Jab becomes indispensable and institutionally visible; the north yard's three lanes and first ledger page exist |
| **3 — Working Depth** | 8 | Jab-leaning, Callie and Toren co-equal | The Director's office refuses the Caul Hill operation; Rook and Dee begin preparing anyway | Ash ladder stages 5–9: absorption, usable working depth, radiation recall, rotation, venting, and overdraw limits; Jab coaches edge-light economy, Callie trains Ward-to-Edge handoff, Toren works toward Guard | Jab must accept training from the same institution trying to claim his labor; Rook must admit Homura knew a reproducible method he never mastered | A controlled exposure exercise becomes a real emergency when a sealed sample or contaminated worker breaches protocol; Jab and Rook perform the first paired recall | The team proves radiation recall works, but also proves it consumes ash too quickly for an improvised trip | The required ash budget, healer rotation, exposure window, abort line, and coach assignments are calculated; Tonk achieves one small independent movement while Tilda has the bad morning that nearly keeps Callie home | Departure is approved informally or undertaken against a formal refusal; Tonk releases Jab to go, Tilda sends Callie, and Senna sends Toren toward the road she cannot walk |
| **4 — The Rip Road** | 8 | Callie-leaning, Jab and Toren co-equal | The team carries a finite ash reserve beyond the Sag's support perimeter toward Caul Hill | Callie widens Ward around moving people; Toren makes Guard reproducible; Jab completes short recall cycles and returns cleanly to reserve | Every delay now has two prices: the machine clock at Meridian and ash consumed protecting exposed bodies | **Approach fight** (action sequence #2): radiation, unstable ground, and lesser spawn force the first full three-protagonist combination before the party reaches the plant | Jab's sense detects that the new rip is pulsing with a Sustain-like rhythm and that something at its center is recalling loose ash | Ash below plan; at least one carried injury; route home threatened | The part and the rip are confirmed to occupy the same tactical problem; there is no clean salvage-only option |

Callie contains its ash-current with Ward, Toren anchors and severs its physical routes with Guard/Edge, and Jab identifies and interrupts the renewal pulse so the team can kill it together | The creature dies only when all three capabilities land in sequence; its ash replenishes the exhausted Sustain rotation after, never before, the decisive exchange | The machine part, a large but costly ash recovery, injuries that cannot be hand-waved away, and proof the rip is changing | Turn for home with the mission won but the ash ledger and bodies showing its cost |
| **6 — What We Brought Home** | 8 | Even rotation, all three | Return under reduced reserves; the machine and patients are still on the clock | Each protagonist proves the new capability outside the climax: Callie holds others, Jab recalls and rotates without self-erasure, Toren leads without replacing his team | The part repairs the shed; the ash haul and recruit record force Meridian to negotiate residency and defender status from a new position | **Return hold** (action sequence #3, shorter than Caul Hill): the cohort and returning party jointly contain a Sag surge caused by the distant rip's collapse | Demonstrated capability—not a speech—wins a specific, incomplete accord; Jab chooses terms of service instead of being assigned them | The part installed; Tonk's infection state and Tilda's symptom response are shown rather than summarized; Senna continues her cycle; mission injuries require recovery; ash is apportioned and obligations recorded | The company takes its name; the three protagonists remain together for Book Three; the north-yard ledger and three practice lanes remain after them as the first seed of the future schools; Cinder's remnant and one disk clue stay as horizon threads |

## 9. Three substantial action sequences

1. **The Sag-line failure (Movement 2).** Tactical problem: a held line under
   direct, close-range pressure, where the danger is a lapse of seconds, not
   raw threat scale — the same problem the ash-collection draft and Ch. 27–28
   already establish. What's different here from Book One's fights: this is
   *training* breaking in real time, not a road ambush; the tension is
   "who's watching whom," and the cost should land on the cohort, making the
   danger of the youth-defender program real rather than theoretical.
2. **The rip-road approach (Movement 4).** Tactical problem: an advancing
   party has to protect its own healers while every pause consumes ash. The
   threat is not only attack; it is being delayed until the Sustain budget
   fails. Callie's moving Ward, Toren's Guard, and Jab's recall first become a
   repeatable team cycle here.
3. **The Caul Hill Sustain-echo (Movement 5).** Tactical problem: attrition
   helps the enemy. The new Riftspawn recalls loose ash and uses it to repair
   itself and nearby spawn. Callie must isolate the current, Toren must make a
   stable physical opening, and Jab must sense and interrupt the renewal
   pulse. The adults keep the radiation lane survivable, but the three
   protagonists solve the fight. Movement 6's Sag hold is consequence and
   public proof, not a second larger climax competing with Caul Hill.

## 10. Source-integration plan

- **Hobb's Wall Ch. 27–28** (Senna's treatment cost, the Director's summons,
  Rook's four-season debt, the disk's "released to bearer" reveal): retain
  in full as Movement 1's spine for Toren's viewpoint strand. The Rook
  ash-collection scene slots in immediately after Ch. 28's debt-signing beat,
  giving the reader the technique before the book needs it dramatically.
- **Callie 18–20** (Tilda's diagnosis, Callie's residency pressure,
  Senna/Tilda contact, Katori recognizing Satori): retain in full as
  Movement 1's Callie strand, reseamed to run concurrently with the Ch. 27–28
  material rather than sequentially — the two wards, side by side, should be
  cross-cut so the reader experiences both diagnoses in the same emotional
  window, matching the book's promise of one shared Meridian opening rather
  than three stacked chronologies.
- **Jab 11–14** (Tonk's diagnosis and surgery, the permanent cost to his leg,
  Jab's contract pressure, the residency question): retain in full as
  Movement 1's Jab strand, cross-cut the same way. Jab's unresolved decision
  about the ash-lifting-equivalent contract (left open at Ch. 14's close)
  should be the thread that carries him into Movement 2's cohort formation —
  his "different heap" promise to Tonk (Ch. 14) is an excellent, already-
  written seed for exactly that.
- **Causal handoffs to add, not already in the source:** the three wards'
  scenes currently touch only lightly (Callie and Toren meet on the bench;
  Senna and Tilda meet across the ward; Jab is adjacent but largely
  unconnected to Callie and Toren directly in this pool). Recommend adding
  brief connective beats — Callie's, Jab's, and Toren's reception-yard and
  ward-corridor timelines already nearly overlap in the existing prose; a
  light reseam can let each one's declaration-card scene and each doctor's
  visit register as happening in the same institutional day, tightening the
  "one shared opening" feel without rewriting any retained scene's content.
- **All nine chapters are strong, reviewed-once first drafts**, per the
  owner's standing note that the Callie and Jab material specifically is not
  yet considered complete. Recommend a continuity and reader pass on the
  Callie and Jab strands before this movement is drafted forward, matching
  the caution already recorded in `COUNTDOWN_WEAVE.md`.

## 11. Plants and payoffs, state-ledger updates, continuity risks, reserved truths

### Plant/payoff table

| Plant | Source | Book 2 payoff |
|---|---|---|
| "One machine... I don't know of another within four hundred miles" (Ch. 28) | Ch. 28 | The book's ticking clock and the reason for the Caul Hill mission |
| Senna's exposure history at Caul Hill (Ch. 27) | Ch. 27 | The mission's destination and its full-circle emotional weight |
| Callie's twin-kindled Ward as a *personal* shield (`UNIVERSE_BIBLE.md`) | Book 1 canon | Tested and confirmed at scale for the first time |
| Jab's "second thing," unnamed, unreliable, and already capable of sensing and stabilizing Tonk | Jab 3, 7, 11–14 | Named as Sustain; trained into deliberate sensing, radiation recall, and ash-funded medical rotation; makes Jab Meridian's primary recruit |
| Toren's Edge+Stride blend, unconfirmed whether repeatable (Ch. 26, prior book) | Prior book | Confirmed, then exceeded — Guard |
| Sustain-echo Riftspawn held in reserve in the universe rules | `UNIVERSE_BIBLE.md` | The Caul Hill creature recalls loose ash to repair itself and other spawn; defeated only by coordinated Ward, Guard/Edge, and Sustain timing |
| The soul holds source-power; ash is ownerless clean fuel | `UNIVERSE_BIBLE.md` | Dee teaches increased usable working depth: more safe ash cycles, not an instant rank increase or a separate storage organ |
| "Released to bearer," no name attached (Ch. 28) | Ch. 28 | One clue, landed in Movement 5 or 6, not the book's engine |
| Rook's four-season ash-lifting debt (Ch. 28) | Ch. 28 | The daily stakes underneath Movements 1–2; his refusal of a shortcut is tested again at Caul Hill |
| Tull alive, Cinder Company "will re-form under another name" (prior book) | Prior book | Confirmed off-page in Movement 6, opening Book 3 |
| Jab's promise to Tonk to "find a different heap" (Ch. 14) | Jab 14 | His route into the cohort and into the found-family's shared obligation |
| Jab and Tonk's cord; the wooden fish that went into surgery | Jab 11–14 | Tonk releases Jab's end at the Caul Hill departure and gives him a physical promise to return, rather than being abandoned or carried again |
| Tilda's "stop holding" lesson and refusal to give Callie the last sum | Callie 10–12, 19 | Tilda sends Callie on the mission without surrendering authority over her own illness; Callie learns that leaving can be an act of trust rather than neglect |
| Senna can never walk another road but remains the group's best memory of Caul Hill | Hobb's Wall 27–28 | She builds the first route/exposure record and seeds the training yard's institutional memory |
| Jab's stable lit fish knife, Toren's ugly spike, Callie's hooked Ward/Edge | Three Book 1 strands | One-obstacle/three-answers curriculum establishes that shared doors do not produce interchangeable fighters |
| The north yard begins keeping repeatable records | Book 2, Movement 2 | First durable seed of Meridian's 2680 door schools and rift-response traditions |

### State-ledger updates required at drafting

Append-only, per `STATE_LEDGER.md` convention — this map does not write the
entries itself, but flags what Movement 1 must establish as opening state
for Book 2: Senna's, Tilda's, and Tonk's confirmed post-diagnosis conditions;
the recovery limits on Rook, Dessa, Wyck, Toren, and Callie; all three
households' residency status; Rook's signed debt; Milo's death as settled grief
entering the book, not news; Callie's and Jab's Ember/reserve states at intake
(both drawn down, per their own cards). Each movement then appends one concrete
medical change for the primary patients and any change in mission fitness for
the recovering party. Do not let a chapter silently reset a body to healthy.

### Continuity risks

- **The Hobb's-Wall draft's "no power inside the wall" rule is not in
  `CANON.md`.** If the owner wants it, it needs to be confirmed as canon
  before Movement 2 depends on it; this map has not assumed it, and instead
  makes the north yard (not "outside the wall entirely") the site where
  supervised training happens, consistent with what Ch. 27–28 already show
  (the north yard as the place "where the work is").
- **Antagonist-figure naming diverges** between the two prior drafts
  (Director Corliss vs. Registrar Helen Quist/Proctor Nye). This map follows
  Director Corliss; if the owner has since preferred the other edition's
  names, that is a one-line fix, not a structural one.
- **Wyck's and Dessa's Book 2 arcs, as drafted in both prior sessions, assumed
  a growth budget this map redirects toward Callie and Jab.** Wyck's and
  Dessa's own material (his Resilience ceiling, her "not just support" arc)
  is worth keeping alive in this book as cohort-level beats, scaled down from
  co-protagonist weight to supporting-cast weight, rather than cut outright.
- **Radiation recall prevents or removes an active burden; it does not erase
  fixed injury.** This protects Senna's established condition and prevents
  Sustain from becoming universal retroactive healing. Exposure windows,
  dosimetry, contamination controls, and ordinary medicine still matter.
- **Usable working depth is not a free power increase.** Training lets a soul
  safely hold and cycle more ash, but intake time, venting, overdraw, physical
  carriage, and the finite ash supply remain hard constraints.
- **The new Riftspawn cannot generate infinite ash.** It recalls ash already
  loosened in the fight or leaking from the rip; once Callie's Ward isolates
  that current and Jab interrupts its Sustain pulse, Toren's damage stays done.
- **Tilda's growth is not repaired by the machine part or by Sustain.** The
  machine supports imaging/treatment infrastructure; her medicines reduce
  swelling and fits. The inoperable growth and her right-side deficits remain.
- **Tonk cannot be mission-portable.** A four-year-old with pins through a
  recently infected fracture does not go to Caul Hill in the old carrying
  frame. His departure scene must preserve his agency without defeating the
  medical facts that made the journey necessary.
- **The 2680 institutions are a lineage seed, not present-day canon pasted
  backward.** Book Two establishes the yard, three methods, and records. Names,
  charters, rivalries, and claims about who founded what remain future-story
  decisions and must reconcile with the final Book Two prose before they are
  promoted from `HORIZON`.

### Reserved truths (deliberately not resolved this book)

- Rook's real name is already delivered (Ch. 27); his third door
  (Resilience) and full unbound status stay open.
- The disk's true origin stays permanently unexplained; this book gets one
  clue, not a resolution.
- Cinder Company's new name/shape: recommend holding it for Book 3's opening
  rather than revealing it on-page here, per both prior drafts' shared
  agreement.
- Whether Director Corliss is a recurring ally, obstacle, or both in Book 3
  stays open.

## 12. Owner decisions still genuinely required

1. **Dee Wren's age and exact door count.** Dee is already canonically the
   Homura survivor Rook walked to safety. Book Two uses Dee as the cross-medical
   teacher rather than inventing a second survivor. Keep age and exact door
   count undisclosed until the owner sets them or the story truly requires them.
2. **Who first identifies Caul Hill.** The destination and merged mission are
   now owner-selected. Recommend Senna name the place herself so the story
   does not use her history without her agency, but the scene staging remains
   open.
3. **The new Meridian-native cohort member's identity** — recommended above
   to keep the cohort from reading as "the Book One cast plus scenery,"
   entirely open for invention at drafting.
4. **Which household carries Movement 5–6's named residency injustice**, and
   whether it is the displaced family found at Caul Hill or a separate
   Meridian household — both work; the owner may prefer to reserve this for
   the drafting author's discretion rather than lock it here.
5. **Whether any romantic thread is wanted** across Callie, Jab, and Toren.
   Book 1's tonal register keeps romance secondary if present at all
   (`CANON.md`, Audience and tone); this map has not assumed one exists.
6. **Confirm or reject the working title "Meridian" against "The Ledger".**
   The map retains *Meridian* because that is the owner's stated Book Two
   title; only a later direct ruling should change it.

## 13. Recommended final book length

**Owner target: approximately 300,000 words.** Movement One established the
actual scale at 39,707 words across eight chapters. Keep six major movements,
use eight-chapter creative runs for Movements Two through Six, and allow the
remaining movements approximately 48,000–55,000 words each when their scenes
earn that room. The resulting forty-eight-chapter book should land roughly in
the 285,000–315,000-word range. This is an allocation, not a padding quota:
spend the added room on developed teaching, medical progression, relationships,
travel consequence, and trackable action rather than repeated explanation.
